378 — Post-Quantum Crypto (Lattice KEM/Signature) from Scratch

Project Number: 378

Project Name: Post-Quantum Crypto (Lattice KEM/Signature) from Scratch

Owner: Mujir Qureshi

Document: FINAL_BLUEPRINT.md

Status: specification — locked

1. Goal

1.1 What Is Built

A from-scratch Rust implementation of two NIST post-quantum standards:

  • ML-KEM (FIPS 203): Module-LWE key encapsulation mechanism, three parameter sets (ML-KEM-512, ML-KEM-768, ML-KEM-1024), CPA-secure PKE lifted to CCA2 KEM via the Fujisaki-Okamoto transform.
  • ML-DSA (FIPS 204): Module-SIS digital signature scheme (Dilithium lineage), three parameter sets (ML-DSA-44, ML-DSA-65, ML-DSA-87), Fiat-Shamir-with-aborts construction.

Both schemes share the same ring R_q = Z_q[X]/(X^256+1), the same NTT-based polynomial multiplier, and the same SHAKE/SHA3 symmetric core.

System boundaries (in scope):

Boundary Included
Primitive layer Zq arithmetic (Montgomery + Barrett), polynomial ring, NTT, SHA3/SHAKE, samplers, bit-packing
Scheme layer ML-KEM CPA PKE + FO KEM; ML-DSA keygen, sign, verify
Constant-time All secret-touching paths; FO ciphertext comparison; zeroization on drop
Conformance Byte-exact KAT against FIPS 203/204 ACVP vectors for all 6 parameter sets
CLI keygen, encaps, decaps, sign, verify, kat subcommands

Explicit non-goals (deferred or out of scope):

Non-goal Gate
AVX2 / AVX-512 / NEON vectorized NTT PhaseDeferred(DF-03, DF-04)
SLH-DSA (FIPS 205, hash-based signatures) PhaseDeferred(DF-01)
Hybrid classical+PQC TLS integration PhaseDeferred(DF-08)
Hardware-accelerated SHA3 (VAES / SHA-NI) PhaseDeferred(DF-13)
Formal verification / proof tooling (ct-verif / binsec) PhaseDeferred(DF-06)
Certified embedded HSM deployment (PKCS#11 interface) PhaseDeferred(DF-12)
ML-KEM-768 / ML-DSA-65 as default API Phase 13 defines public API; default selected then

1.2 Phase 0 Deliverables — Locked Crates

The Phase 0 deliverable is a Cargo workspace containing eleven locked crates:

  1. pqc-core — parameter sets, Zq, KemParams, SignParams, Domain, shared error types
  2. pqc-poly — ring R_q: Poly, PolyVec, PolyMat; add, sub, schoolbook multiply, reduce
  3. pqc-ntt — forward/inverse NTT; twiddle-factor tables; pointwise mul; Montgomery/Barrett mod q
  4. pqc-hash — Keccak-f[1600], SHA3-256/512, SHAKE128/256 XOF; the named functions G, H, PRF, XOF
  5. pqc-sample — CBD sampler, uniform-rejection sampler, matrix/vector seed expansion
  6. pqc-encode — bit-packing (de)serialization, Compress_q/Decompress_q, Dilithium pack/unpack
  7. pqc-kem — CPA PKE (keygen/enc/dec) + CCA2 FO KEM (keygen/encaps/decaps)
  8. pqc-sign — ML-DSA keygen, sign (Fiat-Shamir with aborts), verify (norm + hint checks)
  9. pqc-ct — ct_eq, conditional move/select, zeroization, no-secret-branch guarantees
  10. pqc-kat — ACVP vector loader, deterministic-RNG hook, byte-exact comparison harness
  11. pqc-cli — keygen | encaps | decaps | sign | verify | kat subcommands

1.3 What Later Phases Unlock

Phase range Unlocks
Phases 1–3 Zq, polynomial ring, NTT — foundational arithmetic proving correctness
Phases 4–6 SHA3/SHAKE, samplers, encoding — complete symmetric + encoding layer
Phases 7–8 ML-KEM CPA + CCA2; encaps/decaps with FO implicit rejection
Phases 9–11 ML-DSA full keygen/sign/verify with rejection-sampling loop
Phase 12 Constant-time hardening across all secret paths
Phase 13 KAT conformance, finalized public API
Phase 14 Fuzz, timing tests, perf benchmarks, side-channel review, crate packaging

1.4 Recommended Language: Rust

Lattice crypto is constant-time polynomial arithmetic over Z_q. The NTT, sampling, and modular reduction must run with no secret-dependent branch or memory access, and the output must match the FIPS known-answer-test vectors byte-for-byte. Rust gives fixed-width modular arithmetic, no GC-induced timing variation, exhaustive enums for parameter sets, and the control needed to avoid secret-dependent branching and to zeroize secrets; SHA-3/SHAKE is the symmetric core. The polynomial/NTT kernel, samplers, encoders, ML-KEM, ML-DSA, and constant-time utilities are all Rust; AVX2/NEON vectorization of the NTT is an explicit later optimization, not Phase 0.

1.5 Estimated Timeline

Scope Duration
Phase 0 (complete Phase 0 deliverables above) 6–14 weeks
Full implementation through Phase 14 5–9 months

1.6 Required Prerequisites

Prerequisite Specific knowledge needed
Abstract algebra Polynomial rings, ideal quotient rings Z[X]/(f(X)), ring homomorphisms
Number theory Primitive roots mod q, NTT primitive root selection, CRT
Modular arithmetic Montgomery form, Barrett reduction, lazy reduction, intermediate overflow bounds
Lattice cryptography LWE, MLWE, SIS, shortest/closest vector intuition, hardness assumptions
Symmetric crypto SHA-3, Keccak sponge construction, SHAKE XOF, domain separation
CT programming Branch elimination, cmov patterns, zeroization, compiler barrier idioms
FIPS documents FIPS 203 sec. 4–7 (ML-KEM), FIPS 204 sec. 5–8 (ML-DSA) — must read before Phase 7
Rust Unsafe where needed for CT barriers, zeroize crate, const generics for param sets

2. Why Build This

2.1 Unique Systems Knowledge This Teaches

Domain Specific skill acquired
Lattice mathematics Polynomial ring arithmetic, NTT over Z_q, negacyclic convolution theorem, X^256 = -1
Constant-time engineering Eliminating secret branches at the source level, not just the hardware level; auditing generated assembly
Cryptographic reduction theory Understanding why FO lifts CPA to CCA2; why Fiat-Shamir aborts preserve zero-knowledge
Precision arithmetic Montgomery/Barrett reduction for two different q values (3329, 8380417); intermediate bound tracking
Specification compliance Implementing against a normative FIPS standard with byte-exact KAT validation; differences between spec and an "almost correct" implementation
Rejection sampling Loop-based signing with probabilistic termination; bounding expected iterations; correctness proof sketch
Hint mechanics HighBits/LowBits/UseHint/MakeHint — carry-correction arithmetic for Dilithium verification without t0
Side-channel awareness Timing oracle attacks on KEM (decaps timing distinguishes valid from invalid); Bleichenbacher-style lattice analogues

2.2 Historical and Architectural Significance

  • NIST PQC standardization (2016–2024) is the largest cryptographic migration since RSA.
  • ML-KEM and ML-DSA are the primary NIST-selected algorithms; TLS 1.3, X.509, SSH, and DNSSEC are all migrating.
  • The NTT over Z_q with q=3329 is the narrowest-prime NTT in production cryptography; it exposes Barrett/Montgomery tradeoffs in a minimal arena.
  • Fujisaki-Okamoto is the canonical technique for KEM CCA2 hardening; present in HPKE, TLS-KEMTLS, and post-quantum hybrid protocols.
  • Module-LWE generalises ring-LWE and plain-LWE in a single framework; studying it illuminates the security-efficiency tradeoff across all three.

2.3 Engineering Muscles Developed

  • Algorithm-to-spec discipline: translating pseudocode (FIPS 203 Algorithm 12, 13, etc.) to correct Rust, then validating byte-exact.
  • Parametric generics: encoding multiple security levels (k, eta, du, dv) as const-generic parameters without runtime dispatch overhead.
  • Differential testing: schoolbook vs. NTT multiplication as a correctness oracle (P5); a pattern applicable to any fast-path vs. reference pairing.
  • CT auditing workflow: reading compiler output (cargo rustc -- --emit=asm), using ct-verif or dudect patterns to detect leakage.
  • Fuzz harness construction: structure-aware fuzzing for encoded ciphertext/signature formats; detecting panic/OOB on malformed input.
  • Benchmark-driven micro-optimisation: profiling NTT butterfly count, Montgomery overhead, sampling throughput before and after optimisations.

2.4 Production Engineering Concepts Exercised

Concept Exercise in this project
Specification compliance FIPS 203/204 ACVP KAT: byte-exact outputs, no rounding freedom
Constant-time execution FO comparison, key selection, CBD sampling, zeroization
Probabilistic protocol termination Dilithium signing loop: expected ~4–7 retries; worst-case handling
Implicit failure mode Decaps on tampered ciphertext returns PRF(z,c), indistinguishable from valid
Parameter-set polymorphism Same algorithm, six concrete parameter sets; no copy-paste
Serialization correctness Bit-packing at non-byte-aligned widths (10-bit, 12-bit coefficients)
Fuzz-driven hardening Malformed-byte injection into every deserialization path

2.5 Cross-Links to Portfolio Projects

Portfolio project Relationship
#88 SHA-256/SHA-3 pqc-hash is the Keccak/SHAKE implementation; reuse or re-implement with cross-validation
#87 AES, #89 RSA, #90 Curve25519 Classical-crypto siblings being post-quantum-proofed; architectural contrast
#370 Bignum / modular arithmetic Montgomery/Barrett patterns from bignum apply directly to Zq; shared reduction theory
#92 zk-SNARK NTT over a prime field is the same primitive; polynomial commitment schemes share ring arithmetic
#258 CSS Selector Engine / #259 JS Parser Parser robustness patterns apply to malformed-KAT-vector handling

2.6 Calibration Value

This project occupies the intersection of abstract algebra, constant-time systems programming, and FIPS compliance engineering — a combination rare in practice. Engineers who complete it can:

  • Read and implement NIST cryptographic standards from first principles.
  • Produce CT code auditable at the assembly level.
  • Apply NTT-based fast polynomial arithmetic to adjacent domains (zk-proofs, FHE).
  • Reason about security reductions (CPA→CCA2, Fiat-Shamir→signature security) as implementation constraints, not just theory.

2.7 What Mastery Looks Like

  • Reduction-chain ownership (Phase 1–3): given any lattice-based scheme, you can derive the Barrett/Montgomery constants from scratch, verify the zeta table primitive-root selection, and prove the NTT round-trip identity before touching higher-level code.
  • Spec-to-bit-pack fluency (Phase 6–7): you can read FIPS 203 Appendix A or FIPS 204 sec. 5, identify every bit-width and endianness decision, and produce byte-exact encoding without a reference implementation — then catch a rounding-direction bug by inspection.
  • FO-transform security argument (Phase 8): you can explain why implicit rejection (PRF(z,c)) achieves IND-CCA2 in the QROM, why ct_select is the only safe conduit for the comparison result, and why a single early-return breaks the security proof.
  • Rejection-sampling ZK invariant (Phase 10): you can trace why aborting preserves the zero-knowledge property of Fiat-Shamir-with-aborts, bound the expected iteration count from the norm parameters, and identify any code path that would correlate z with s1.
  • Constant-time audit workflow (Phase 12): you can inspect LLVM IR or objdump output for a given secret-touching function, identify every conditional branch or table-index that could leak, replace them with ct_select/ct_cmov, and run dudect to confirm the statistical t-test passes.
  • ACVP conformance engineering (Phase 13): you can set up a deterministic RNG shim, drive all six parameter sets through the NIST ACVP JSON vector format, and produce a diff-on-failure log that names the diverging byte offset and the algorithm step responsible.
  • Fuzz-driven correctness assurance (Phase 14): you can design structure-aware fuzz harnesses for each public entry point, seed them from KAT vectors plus single-bit-flip mutations, interpret libFuzzer crash artifacts, and distinguish a security-relevant panic from a benign assertion failure.

3. Architectural Overview

3.1 Canonical Architecture Diagram

                          POST-QUANTUM CRYPTO  (ML-KEM + ML-DSA, module lattices)
  +------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
  |   pqc-cli (keygen / encaps / decaps / sign / verify / kat)                           |
  |        |                                            |                                |
  |        v                                            v                                |
  |   pqc-kem (ML-KEM)                              pqc-sign (ML-DSA)                      |
  |   CPA PKE + Fujisaki-Okamoto -> CCA2 KEM        Fiat-Shamir w/ aborts + rejection     |
  |        |            |            |                  |          |        |             |
  |        v            v            v                  v          v        v             |
  |   pqc-sample    pqc-encode   (re-encrypt        pqc-sample  pqc-encode  norm/hint      |
  |   (CBD,         (compress,    on decaps)        (uniform/    (pack)     checks         |
  |    rejection)    pack)                           CBD)                                 |
  |        |            |                               |          |                      |
  |        +-----+------+---------------+---------------+----------+                      |
  |              |                      |                                                 |
  |              v                      v                                                 |
  |   pqc-ntt (forward/inverse NTT, pointwise)   pqc-poly (R_q add/sub/mul, reduce)        |
  |              |                      |                                                 |
  |              +----------+-----------+                                                 |
  |                         v                                                             |
  |   pqc-hash (SHA3 + SHAKE: G, H, PRF, XOF — seed expansion + hashing)                   |
  |                         |                                                             |
  |                         v                                                             |
  |   pqc-ct (constant-time eq / cmov / zeroize)   pqc-kat (FIPS 203/204 ACVP vectors)     |
  +------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

3.2 Subsystem Descriptions

pqc-cli — Entry point. Parses subcommands (keygen, encaps, decaps, sign, verify, kat), reads/writes PEM-like or raw binary key/message/ciphertext/signature files. No cryptographic logic; delegates entirely to pqc-kem, pqc-sign, pqc-kat. Trust boundary: untrusted file input; validates before passing to scheme layer. No concurrency; sequential per invocation.

pqc-kem — Implements ML-KEM. Two layers: (1) CPA PKE (keygen, enc, dec) over Module-LWE using pqc-sample for key and noise polynomials, pqc-ntt for matrix-vector products, pqc-encode for Compress/Decompress and byte packing. (2) CCA2 KEM wrapping CPA PKE via the Fujisaki-Okamoto transform: encaps hashes message + pk-hash through G to get (K, r), calls enc(m; r); decaps decrypts, re-encrypts, performs constant-time ciphertext comparison (via pqc-ct), returns K or PRF(z, c). Secret: sk (s, pk-hash, z). Trust boundary: all ciphertext bytes treated as adversarial. No I/O; all data passed by value or slice.

pqc-sign — Implements ML-DSA. keygen expands A from rho, samples s1/s2 via pqc-sample, computes t = A*s1 + s2, splits t into (t1, t0). sign runs the Fiat-Shamir-with-aborts loop: sample masking y, compute w = A*y, derive challenge c from H(tr, msg, w1), compute z = y + c*s1, check norm bounds and reject if violated, compute hint h. verify recomputes w1' via UseHint, recomputes c', checks c' == c~ and norm bounds. Uses pqc-ntt, pqc-sample, pqc-encode, pqc-ct. Secret: sk (s1, s2, t0, y during signing). Signing loop is probabilistic; expected retries bounded by rejection probability.

pqc-sample — Stateless samplers driven by pqc-hash XOF output. cbd_eta(prf_stream, eta) — centered binomial: constant-time, output is secret. uniform_rej(xof_stream) — rejection sampling mod q for matrix A coefficients: variable-time (output is public). expand_a(rho) — generates the K×L public matrix A by calling XOF per (i,j). expand_s(sigma, nonce) — expands secret/noise vectors. Input: seed bytes. Output: Poly or PolyVec. No persistent state.

pqc-encode — Deterministic (de)serialization. ML-KEM: Compress_q(x, d) rounds x to d bits; Decompress_q(y, d) reconstructs; ByteEncode/ByteDecode at d bits/coefficient. ML-DSA: pack_t1, pack_t0, pack_s, pack_z, pack_h, unpack_*. All functions are pure (no side effects); input validation returns Err on wrong length. Roundtrip invariant: decode(encode(x)) == x for lossless cases; compression error bounded by FIPS spec.

pqc-ntt — Core multiplier. Precomputed twiddle-factor table ZETAS: [i32; 128] (primitive 256th root of unity mod q). Forward NTT: 7-layer Cooley-Tukey butterfly in-place; inverse NTT: Gentleman-Sande, includes the n^{-1} normalisation factor folded into the last layer. Pointwise multiplication: element-wise product in NTT domain with Montgomery reduction. Invariants I5. All arithmetic is data-independent; no secret values in this crate (A expansion uses it but A is public; KEM/sign pass NTT-domain polys without secret-conditional branching here).

pqc-poly — Reference ring layer. Poly.add, Poly.sub — coefficient-wise mod q. Poly.schoolbook_mul — O(n²) negacyclic multiply (X^256 = -1); used only for differential testing against pqc-ntt (P5). Poly.reduce_coeffs — ensures all coefficients in [0, q). PolyVec and PolyMat wrappers with vectorised add/inner-product. Domain enum tags each Poly instance.

pqc-hash — Keccak-f[1600] permutation, hand-unrolled for constant-time and performance; no lookup tables that could cause cache-timing leakage. Exports: sha3_256, sha3_512, shake128_xof, shake256_xof with streaming squeeze interface. Named functions per FIPS: G = SHA3-512, H = SHA3-256, PRF = SHAKE256, XOF = SHAKE128. Validated against NIST SHA-3 KAT vectors independently of pqc-kat.

pqc-ct — Constant-time utilities. ct_eq(a, b) -> u8 — returns 1 iff equal, no branch. ct_select(cond, a, b) — branchless mux. ct_cmov(dst, src, cond) — conditional copy. zeroize(buf) — compiler-barrier-guarded memory wipe. ct_compare_bytes(a, b) -> u8 — constant-time byte-slice comparison used by FO. This crate has no dependencies on other pqc-* crates; all others depend on it.

pqc-kat — KAT harness. Reads FIPS 203/204 ACVP JSON/text vectors (keyed by algorithm + parameter set). Provides a deterministic-RNG shim (seeds the samplers deterministically per KAT entry). Drives pqc-kem and pqc-sign through each test vector. Asserts byte-exact equality of public keys, secret keys, ciphertexts, shared secrets, and signatures. Enforces invariant I1. Fails immediately on first mismatch with a diff showing expected vs. actual hex.

4. System Constraints

4.1 Quantified Engineering Targets

Constraint Target Notes
Memory — KEM keygen < 8 KB stack ML-KEM-768: pk=1184 B, sk=2400 B; intermediate polys transient
Memory — KEM encaps/decaps < 16 KB stack Includes re-encryption buffer on decaps
Memory — DSA keygen < 16 KB stack ML-DSA-65: pk=1952 B, sk=4032 B
Memory — DSA sign < 32 KB stack Masking vector y + w per loop iteration; worst-case 4–7 iterations
Memory — NTT tables 1 KB static (256 × i32) Twiddle table; read-only; cache-resident
Heap allocation Zero per operation All key/ciphertext/signature buffers caller-supplied or stack
KEM-768 keygen throughput ≥ 10 000 ops/s on modern x86-64 Single-threaded; no SIMD
KEM-768 encaps throughput ≥ 10 000 ops/s
KEM-768 decaps throughput ≥ 8 000 ops/s Re-encryption adds ~1 NTT call overhead vs. encaps
DSA-65 keygen throughput ≥ 2 000 ops/s More NTT ops than KEM keygen
DSA-65 sign throughput ≥ 1 500 ops/s Amortised over expected rejection iterations
DSA-65 verify throughput ≥ 4 000 ops/s No rejection loop; deterministic
NTT latency (single poly, 256 coeffs) < 5 µs p99 Software-only; q=3329
KEM-768 encaps p50 latency < 100 µs Single-threaded
KEM-768 encaps p99 latency < 250 µs No GC; deterministic arithmetic
DSA-65 sign p50 latency < 500 µs Rejection loop expected ~4 iterations
DSA-65 sign p99 latency < 2 ms Worst-case iteration tail
DSA-65 verify p50 latency < 200 µs
KAT suite execution < 60 s All 6 parameter sets, full ACVP vector sets
Startup time (CLI) < 50 ms Table init is compile-time const where possible
Binary size (stripped) < 4 MB All crates; no external crypto dependencies
Concurrency No shared mutable state All operations are stateless functions over caller-supplied buffers
Thread safety All functions Send + Sync No global mutable state outside const tables
Max parameter sets 6 (3 KEM + 3 DSA) Fixed at compile time via const generics
Zeroization latency < 1 µs per secret buffer Compiler-barrier-guarded; not elided
Signing rejection-loop iterations (expected) ≤ 8 average ML-DSA-65; ≥ 1/(rejection probability)
Signing rejection-loop iterations (p99) ≤ 25 Exponential tail; loop must be bounded in practice
Decapsulation failure probability < 2^{-128} Per FIPS 203 sec. 3.3; verified by parameter selection
Deployment model Library crate + CLI binary No daemon, no network I/O in core crates
OS targets Linux x86-64 (primary), macOS arm64 (secondary) No OS-specific syscalls in core; getrandom for keygen RNG
Rust edition 2021 Const generics, array patterns, let-else
MSRV (minimum supported Rust version) 1.75 Stable const generics, [T; N] patterns
External dependencies zeroize (1.x), sha3 optional cross-check; no openssl, no ring Core implementation is from-scratch
FIPS KAT pass rate 100% Zero tolerance for byte-mismatch on any ACVP vector
Fuzz crash budget Zero panics / OOB on any input Applies to all deserialization and verification paths
CT timing variance < 1% coefficient of variation on secret-path benchmarks Measured via dudect-style statistical test
Side-channel audit No secret-dependent branch, table lookup, or division in reviewed paths Documented per Phase 12

5. Required Reading

Books

B1. Introduction to Modern Cryptography — Katz & Lindell, 3rd ed. (CRC Press 2021)

  • Ch. 11 (Public-Key Encryption): IND-CPA and IND-CCA2 game definitions; reduction proofs from DDH/LWE; hybrid encryption pattern (KEM + DEM).
  • Ch. 12 (KEM/DEM separation): formal KEM definition; how encapsulation/decapsulation match the CCA2 oracle; why KEM is the right abstraction for ML-KEM.
  • Ch. 13 (Digital Signatures): Fiat-Shamir transform (NI-ZK → signature); strong unforgeability; hash-based instantiation.
  • Extract: security notions (IND-CCA2, sEUF-CMA) that ML-KEM and ML-DSA are proven to satisfy; these become the design goals for Phase 7–8 and Phase 10–11.
  • Applied in: §6 (P3, P4 security definitions), Phase 7 §7.1 objectives, Phase 8 §8.1 objectives, Phase 10 §10.1 ZK argument.

B2. Post-Quantum Cryptography — Bernstein & Lange (eds.), Springer 2009

  • Ch. 3 (Lattice-based cryptography): SVP/CVP/BDD; Regev LWE; ring variant; Module-LWE as a middle ground between plain-LWE efficiency and Ring-LWE assumptions.
  • Ch. 2 (Security survey): LLL/BKZ basis reduction; concrete security estimates vs. BKZ-β blocksize.
  • Appendix A: parameter selection methodology; failure probability formula: Pr[failure] = Pr[\|v - u^T s\|_∞ > q/4].
  • Extract: why η=2 and η=3 for different ML-KEM parameter sets; how k=2/3/4 trades security level vs ciphertext size.
  • Applied in: §4 (System Constraints — decapsulation failure probability ≤ 2^{-128}), §7 Invariant I3, Phase 7 §7.1 security rationale, Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model (failure-probability derivation).

B3. Serious Cryptography — Aumasson, No Starch Press 2018

  • Ch. 12 (SHA-3 and Keccak): Keccak-f[1600] five-step round function (θ, ρ, π, χ, ι); sponge absorb/squeeze; XOF output extension; rate/capacity security argument.
  • Ch. 6 (Diffie-Hellman): KEM encapsulation / decapsulation pattern; ephemeral key generation.
  • Extract: SHAKE128 rate r=1344 bits, capacity c=256 bits; SHAKE256 rate r=1088, c=512; padding byte 0x1F vs SHA-3's 0x06; squeeze loop for arbitrary-length output feeding pqc-sample.
  • Applied in: Phase 4 §4.2 (pqc-hash Keccak implementation), §8.6 NTT domain transition model (SHAKE XOF seed expansion), Mental Models MM4 (sponge construction insight).

B4. Cryptography Engineering — Ferguson, Schneier, Kohno, 2nd ed. (Wiley 2010)

  • Ch. 5 (Implementation pitfalls): branch-on-secret; compiler optimisation of dead writes; integer overflow; lookup-table timing.
  • Ch. 17 (Side-channel attacks): timing, power, cache, fault injection; defensive patterns (constant-time, masking, blinding).
  • Ch. 20 (Key management): key lifetime; secure storage; zeroization requirements.
  • Extract: the "defense in depth" argument for combining constant-time arithmetic (P1) + zeroization (P8) + strict error handling (P9); directly maps to Phase 12 audit checklist.
  • Applied in: §6 P1, P8, P9 (principle formulation), Phase 12 §12.2 audit checklist, Phase 14 §14.2 side-channel review table.

B5. Handbook of Applied Cryptography (HAC) — Menezes, van Oorschot, Vanstone (online, 1996)

  • HAC sec. 14.2 (Modular arithmetic): multiple-precision integers; reduction algorithms.
  • Applied in: Phase 1 §1.2 (Barrett and Montgomery algorithm descriptions), Phase 3 §3.2 (zeta table derivation), §8.8 reduction model.
  • HAC sec. 14.3 (Montgomery multiplication): MonPro(a,b) = a*b*R^{-1} mod q; the REDC algorithm; Montgomery entry aR mod q, exit a mod q.
  • HAC sec. 14.4 (Barrett reduction): Barrett_Reduce(a): precomputed floor(R/q) allows constant-time approximate division.
  • Extract: exact REDC pseudocode for pqc-ntt Montgomery butterfly; select R = 2^16 for q=3329 (fits in 16-bit); for q=8380417 select R = 2^23 or 2^32.

B6. Guide to Elliptic Curve Cryptography — Hankerson, Menezes, Vanstone (Springer 2004)

  • B6 sec. 2.2 (Prime field arithmetic): Montgomery domain aR mod p; field entry/exit; multiplication (ab)R^{-1} mod p.
  • B6 sec. 2.3 (Reduction): modular reduction via pre-computed inverses; lazy reduction strategies.
  • Extract: the Montgomery domain approach transfers directly to Zq; NTT twiddle factors are stored in Montgomery form to save one multiplication per butterfly.
  • Applied in: Phase 1 §1.2 (Montgomery constants), Phase 3 §3.2 (Montgomery twiddle encoding in ZETA_KYBER), §8.8 reduction table.

B7. Mathematics of Public Key Cryptography — Galbraith (Cambridge 2012)

  • Ch. 18 (Lattices): basis reduction; BDD decoding; ideal lattices; ring-LWE connection to ideal-LWE.
  • Ch. 19 (Learning with Errors): Regev's reduction; variant hardness assumptions; ring-LWE in R_q.
  • Extract: why X^256 + 1 is the chosen modulus (256th cyclotomic polynomial, splits completely mod q=3329 enabling full NTT); the negacyclic convolution theorem; why φ(n) = 256 gives 256 NTT points.
  • Applied in: §8.1 (R_q definition, Domain Model), §8.6 NTT domain transition model, Mental Models MM1 (module-lattice security intuition), Alternative Architectures (why plain-LWE was not chosen).

Papers

Pa-1. Regev (2005/2009) — "On Lattices, Learning with Errors, Random Linear Codes, and Cryptography"

  • Venue: STOC 2005; J. ACM 56(6) 2009.
  • Contribution: LWE hardness via quantum reduction from GapSVP and SIVP; LWE public-key encryption scheme.
  • Extract: the LWE distribution (A, A*s + e mod q) where e is small-norm Gaussian; the hardness parameter choice q ≈ n^2, σ ≈ sqrt(n).
  • Relevance: theoretical foundation for Module-LWE hardness used in ML-KEM; understanding why small η noise is the right choice.
  • Applied in: §6 P12 (explicit security argument requirement), §8.1 (R_q hardness), Mental Models MM1, Comparative Architecture Table (security assumption column).

Pa-2. Lyubashevsky, Peikert, Regev (2010/2013) — "On Ideal Lattices and LWE over Rings"

  • Venue: EUROCRYPT 2010; J. ACM 60(6) 2013.
  • Contribution: Ring-LWE: LWE in the ring R_q = Z_q[X]/(f(X)); polynomial-time hardness reduction from ideal-lattice problems; NTT-compatible ring choice.
  • Extract: when f(X) = X^n + 1 with n a power of 2 and q ≡ 1 mod 2n, f splits into n linear factors mod q enabling full NTT; for ML-KEM q=3329 and n=256, q ≡ 1 mod 512 is confirmed.
  • Relevance: establishes the ring structure for pqc-poly; justifies the NTT design in pqc-ntt Phase 3.
  • Applied in: Phase 2 §2.2 (negacyclic convolution), Phase 3 §3.2 (NTT layer count and zeta primitive root), §8.6 NTT domain model, Alternative Architectures (Ring-LWE vs Module-LWE).

Pa-3. Bos et al. — "CRYSTALS-Kyber" (NIST PQC Round 3 v3.02, 2021)

  • Venue: NIST PQC Round 3 submission.
  • Contribution: ML-KEM-512/768/1024 parameter sets; Module-LWE CPA PKE; FO-KEM construction; NTT for q=3329 with ω=17.
  • Sections to read: §2 (algorithms, byte encoding); §3 (security analysis, parameter justification); §4 (implementation guidance, NTT layout); Appendix A (known-answer vectors).
  • Relevance: primary normative design reference for pqc-kem; every algorithm step in Phase 7–8 comes from this document.
  • Applied in: Phase 7 §7.2 (CPA PKE step-by-step), Phase 8 §8.2 (FO KEM), §8.2 parameter table, §8.10 worked example, Comparative Architecture Table (liboqs vs this design).

Pa-4. Ducas et al. — "CRYSTALS-Dilithium" (NIST PQC Round 3 v3.1, 2021)

  • Venue: NIST PQC Round 3 submission.
  • Contribution: ML-DSA-44/65/87 parameter sets; Fiat-Shamir-with-aborts signing; HighBits/LowBits decomposition; MakeHint/UseHint hint mechanism.
  • Sections to read: §2 (algorithm specification); §3 (security proofs, rejection sampling analysis); §4 (parameter rationale); Appendix A (KAT seeds).
  • Relevance: primary design reference for pqc-sign Phase 9–11; HighBits/LowBits definitions are the exact functions in pqc-encode.
  • Applied in: Phase 9 §9.2 (keygen), Phase 10 §10.2 (signing loop), Phase 11 §11.2 (verification), §8.5 signing state machine, §7 I7, Back-of-Envelope (DSA signature sizes).

Pa-5. Fujisaki & Okamoto (1999/2013) — "Secure Integration of Asymmetric and Symmetric Encryption Schemes"

  • Venue: CRYPTO 1999; J. Cryptology 26(1) 2013.
  • Contribution: FO transform: CPA encryption scheme → CCA2 by deterministic re-encryption; implicit rejection on mismatch.
  • Extract: the decapsulation path (K, r) = G(m); c' = Enc(pk, m; r); if c' ≠ c then K = PRF(z, c); the crucial point is that the PRF branch is always taken without branching.
  • Relevance: direct theoretical basis for pqc-kem::decaps Phase 8; the implicit rejection mechanism enforcing I4.
  • Applied in: §6 P3 (CCA2 via FO principle), Phase 8 §8.2 (decaps algorithm), §8.4 ML-KEM lifecycle state machine, I4 (implicit rejection invariant), Mental Models MM3.

Pa-6. Hofheinz, Hövelmanns, Kiltz (2017) — "A Modular Analysis of the Fujisaki-Okamoto Transformation"

  • Venue: TCC 2017 (LNCS 10677).
  • Contribution: Decomposing FO into T + U + V sub-transforms; tight ROM/QROM reductions; formal proof that implicit rejection (PRF return) achieves IND-CCA2 in the QROM.
  • Extract: the U^⊥ transform is the implicit-rejection variant; the reduction tightness argument; the QROM bound relevant for post-quantum adversaries.
  • Relevance: confirms that PRF(z,c) is the correctly specified implicit-rejection output; Phase 8 security argument relies on this analysis.
  • Applied in: Phase 8 §8.2 (PRF(z,c) justification), §6 P3 (QROM security argument), Hard Questions HQ-7 (why PRF is the correct fallback, not an error code).

Pa-7. Lyubashevsky (2009) — "Fiat-Shamir with Aborts: Applications to Lattice and Factoring-Based Signatures"

  • Venue: ASIACRYPT 2009 (LNCS 5912).
  • Contribution: Rejection sampling in the Fiat-Shamir transform; the abort-and-retry loop for lattice signatures; zero-knowledge argument for the rejection criterion.
  • Extract: Pr[accept] = min(D_y(z), M * D_{s+y}(z)) / M — the acceptance probability determines expected iterations; the bound γ1 - β is chosen so that M (the bound on the rejection ratio) is small.
  • Relevance: Phase 10: the abort loop is mandatory for security; the norm bounds γ1, β, and ω in SignParams derive from this paper.
  • Applied in: §6 P4 (rejection sampling preserves ZK), Phase 10 §10.2 (signing loop structure), I7 (norm bound invariant), §4 System Constraints (signing rejection-loop iterations ≤ 8 expected).

Pa-8. Longa & Naehrig (2016) — "Speeding up the Number Theoretic Transform for Faster Ideal Lattice-Based Cryptography"

  • Venue: CANS 2016.
  • Contribution: Merged Cooley-Tukey + Gentleman-Sande butterfly with lazy Montgomery reduction; reduces Montgomery reductions per NTT from nlog(n) to nlog(n)/2; zeta table layout for q=3329.
  • Extract: the "lazy" strategy: defer Barrett/Montgomery reduction by allowing inputs in [0, 2q) and reducing only when accumulation would overflow i32; for q=3329, up to 2 un-reduced additions fit in 16-bit.
  • Relevance: the pqc-ntt butterfly loop implements this merged strategy; the precomputed twiddle table ZETAS[256] is laid out in bit-reversed order as described.
  • Applied in: Phase 3 §3.2 (NTT implementation strategy), Phase 1 §1.2 (Montgomery R selection), §8.8 reduction model, Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model (NTT latency derivation).

Pa-9. Avanzi, Bos, Ducas et al. (2022) — "CRYSTALS-Kyber: a CCA-secure module-lattice-based KEM"

  • Venue: IEEE S&P 2022.
  • Contribution: Formal security proof for the complete two-level Kyber scheme; tight ROM reduction from Module-LWE to IND-CCA2 KEM.
  • Extract: the re-encryption must use r = H(m, H(pk)) (the same deterministic derivation as encapsulation); the proof breaks if re-encryption uses a different r.
  • Relevance: confirms the exact G(m, H(pk)) = (K, r) construction in pqc-kem::encaps and pqc-kem::decaps; Phase 8 implementation must match.
  • Applied in: Phase 8 §8.2 (G-split construction), Phase 8 §8.6 pitfall (G split point), Comparative Architecture Table (BoringSSL/Google PQC comparison — same FO structure).

Pa-10. Ducas et al. (2018) — "CRYSTALS-Dilithium: A Lattice-Based Digital Signature Scheme"

  • Venue: TCHES 2018(1).
  • Contribution: HighBits_α(r), LowBits_α(r), MakeHint_α(z,r), UseHint_α(h,r) integer definitions; hint correctness proof; deconstruction of the signing loop.
  • Extract: HighBits(r,α) = (r - r mod α) / (α/2) for α = 2*γ2; LowBits(r,α) = r mod α; UseHint(h,r,α) corrects the high bits using the carry hint bit h.
  • Relevance: exact integer arithmetic for pqc-encode Phase 6; hint computation in Phase 10; verification in Phase 11; these are normatively defined in FIPS 204 but this paper provides the derivation.
  • Applied in: Phase 6 §6.2 (HighBits/LowBits in dilithium_pack.rs), Phase 10 §10.2 (make_hint), Phase 11 §11.2 (use_hint), §8.5 DSA signing state machine.

Pa-11. Kocher (1996) — "Timing Attacks on Implementations of Diffie-Hellman, RSA, DSS, and Other Systems"

  • Venue: CRYPTO 1996 (LNCS 1109).
  • Contribution: First practical timing side-channel; variable-time Montgomery exponentiation leaks secret exponent bits via statistical analysis of modular reduction timing differences.
  • Extract: the attack requires only timing measurements and the public key; applies to any variable-time modular arithmetic on secret inputs.
  • Relevance: motivates P1, I2, Phase 12; the argument extends to polynomial arithmetic in pqc-ntt and the FO comparison in pqc-kem.
  • Applied in: §6 P1 (constant-time mandate), Phase 12 §12.2 (CT audit checklist motivating ct_eq), Threat Model T4, Mental Models MM2.

Pa-12. Bernstein (2005) — "Cache-Timing Attacks on AES"

  • Venue: Preprint, cr.yp.to/antiforgery.
  • Contribution: Cache-timing attack via table-lookup: T-table AES leaks the secret key through cache-hit/miss patterns on lookup tables.
  • Extract: any table indexed by a secret value (even a seemingly innocent 256-byte S-box) leaks ~1 bit per cache miss; cache lines of 64 bytes expose 4 table entries per query.
  • Relevance: pqc-ct must avoid tables indexed by secret; NTT zeta table is indexed by public loop counter (safe); CBD sampling must not use a lookup table.
  • Applied in: §6 P1 (no secret-indexed table), Phase 5 §5.2 (CBD constant-time bit extraction, no lookup), Phase 12 §12.2 (variable-time exceptions documenting NTT zeta table is public), Phase 4 §4.2 (no lookup tables in Keccak).

Pa-13. Becker et al. (2023) — "Masking the Kyber Decapsulation"

  • Venue: CHES 2023.
  • Contribution: First-order masked ML-KEM decapsulation; cost model for masked FO comparison; hardest-to-mask operations identified.
  • Extract: the FO implicit-rejection select (ct_select(K_good, K_bad, eq_bit)) is the highest-cost masking point; requires a 32-byte masked conditional copy.
  • Relevance: context for future Phase 12 extensions; shows where I2 is most expensive to enforce when first-order masking is required.
  • Applied in: Phase 14 §14.2 (side-channel review cost model), Phase 12 §12.2 (ct_cmov design justification), Hard Questions HQ-15 (masking cost tradeoff question).

Pa-14. Bernstein & Yang (2019) — "Fast Constant-Time GCD and Modular Inversion"

  • Venue: TCHES 2019(3).
  • Contribution: divstep algorithm: constant-time GCD/modular inversion via a simple iteration; no secret-dependent branches.
  • Extract: the black_box barrier pattern preventing compiler optimisation of constant-time code; the principle of expressing conditional operations as arithmetic.
  • Relevance: pqc-ct's ct_select and ct_eq implementations follow the same barrier pattern.
  • Applied in: Phase 12 §12.2 (ct_eq_bytes barrier design, compiler fence justification), §6 P1 (black_box pattern cited), Phase 14 §14.2 (LTO-eliminates-ct_eq pitfall).

Reference Implementations

R1. pq-crystals/kyber (C reference implementation)

  • Study: ref/poly.c (coefficient ops), ref/ntt.c (forward/inverse NTT, twiddle table), ref/reduce.c (Montgomery/Barrett for q=3329), ref/symmetric-shake.c (G/H/PRF/XOF wrappers), ref/kem.c (full KEM), ref/indcpa.c (CPA PKE).
  • Lessons: canonical NTT zeta table layout (zetas[128], bit-reversed); FO re-encryption exact byte ordering; CPA keygen/enc/dec byte layout per FIPS 203; KYBER_INDCPA_BYTES / KYBER_PUBLICKEYBYTES constants.
  • Pitfalls: NOT constant-time on all compilers/platforms; the cmov in ref/verify.c is implemented as -(int16_t)(v) which may compile to a branch in MSVC; re-implement all secret paths with pqc-ct barriers.
  • Applied in: Phase 2 §2.2 (NTT zeta layout derivation), Phase 4 §4.2 (twiddle table verification), Phase 7 §7.2 (CPA keygen/enc/dec byte layout, FO re-encryption), §6 P1 pitfall (MSVC cmov branch).

R2. pq-crystals/dilithium (C reference implementation)

  • Study: ref/sign.c (keygen/sign/verify), ref/poly.c + ref/polyvec.c (polynomial arithmetic), ref/ntt.c (NTT for q=8380417, ω=1753), ref/packing.c (sk/pk/sig byte packing), ref/rounding.c (HighBits/LowBits/MakeHint/UseHint).
  • Lessons: hint packing byte format (one byte per hint bit, packed densely); sigma expansion for s1/s2 using poly_uniform_eta; the rejection loop in sign.c:crypto_sign_signature.
  • Pitfalls: variable-time reduce32 in some versions; ref/poly.c::poly_reduce may not be constant-time for secret polys; re-implement with pqc-ct barriers for s1/s2/t0/y paths.
  • Applied in: Phase 3 §3.2 (Montgomery reduction for q=8380417), Phase 8 §8.2 (hint packing format, signing rejection loop), Phase 12 §12.2 (secret poly constant-time barrier requirement).

R3. RustCrypto/ml-kem (Rust crate)

  • Study: src/kem.rs (KEM encaps/decaps), src/pke.rs (CPA PKE), src/encode.rs (byte serialization), src/sampling.rs (CBD, uniform), src/params.rs (parameter set traits).
  • Lessons: generic KemCore<P: KyberParams> over parameter sets; zeroize::Zeroize derive on DecapsKey; subtle::ConstantTimeEq for ciphertext comparison; Rust type-level parameter dispatch.
  • Pitfalls: pre-FIPS-203-final versions used draft-round KAT vectors — verify against final FIPS 203 ACVP before trusting byte layout; do not copy bit-packing code without confirming against FIPS 203 Appendix B.
  • Applied in: Phase 1 §1.2 (workspace crate design, Rust type-level param dispatch), Phase 7 §7.2 (KEM encaps/decaps implementation reference), Phase 13 §13.2 (KAT ACVP version pinning).

R4. RustCrypto/signatures/dilithium (Rust crate)

  • Study: src/sign.rs (signing loop), src/verify.rs (verification with norm checks), src/poly.rs (polynomial ops), src/packing.rs (key/signature packing).
  • Lessons: zeroize on signing intermediate state (SigningKey holds s1/s2 as Zeroizing<...>); subtle::ConstantTimeEq for challenge polynomial comparison; Rust signature trait integration.
  • Pitfalls: parameter encoding may diverge from FIPS 204 final; validate every public-key and signature byte against ACVP before treating as normative.
  • Applied in: Phase 8 §8.2 (signing loop structure, zeroize pattern), Phase 9 §9.2 (verification with norm checks, hint reconstruct), Phase 13 §13.2 (ACVP final vector validation for ML-DSA).

R5. RustCrypto/hashes — sha3 crate

  • Study: sha3/src/keccak.rs (Keccak-f[1600] state and round function), sha3/src/state.rs (sponge absorb/squeeze), sha3/src/lib.rs (SHA3-256/512 and SHAKE128/256 instantiation).
  • Lessons: KeccakState as a [u64; 25] lane array; absorb pads and applies permutation; XofReader for squeeze-on-demand; rate/capacity selection per variant.
  • Pitfalls: SHAKE padding byte is 0x1F; SHA-3 padding byte is 0x06; a copy-paste error here produces silent KAT failures; pqc-hash must verify against FIPS 202 SHA-3 and SHAKE vectors independently.
  • Applied in: Phase 5 §5.2 (pqc-hash crate design, XofReader API, Keccak-f[1600] round function), Phase 13 §13.2 (FIPS 202 KAT verification, padding byte validation), I5 (padding byte invariant).

R6. dalek-cryptography/subtle

  • Study: src/lib.rs entirely: Choice type (wraps u8, opaque to LLVM); ConstantTimeEq trait; ConditionallySelectable trait; ConstantTimeLess / ConstantTimeGreater.
  • Lessons: Choice is #[repr(transparent)] over u8 to prevent the compiler from widening to bool and optimising; the black_box barrier prevents dead-code elimination of the comparison result.
  • Pitfalls: do NOT implement ct_eq as (a == b) as u8 — LLVM compiles this to setne + zero-extend which is often a branch at -O2; use subtle::ConstantTimeEq or replicate its barrier idiom.
  • Applied in: Phase 12 §12.2 (pqc-ct crate design, ct_eq_bytes, ct_select, Choice barrier), §6 P1 (black_box mechanism), Phase 7 §7.2 (FO comparison result ct_select usage).

R7. NIST ACVP Test Server + usnistgov/ACVP-Server

  • Study: gen-val/json-files/ML-KEM/ and gen-val/json-files/ML-DSA/ — JSON test vector files for all parameter sets; each entry has tcId, rng seed, expected ek, dk, m, c, k, sig.
  • Lessons: deterministic DRBG seed format (seeded with a fixed 48-byte value); kat test group structure; intermediate value dumps in extended test vectors.
  • Pitfalls: ACVP draft vectors differ from final FIPS 203/204 vectors in domain-separation bytes; only test against vectors explicitly tagged for the final standard; check the revision field in the capability JSON.
  • Applied in: Phase 13 §13.2 (pqc-kat crate design, ACVP JSON parsing, all 6 parameter-set KATs), Phase 10 §10.2 (initial KAT integration testing), §4 System Constraints (KAT vector set counts).

R8. bwesterb/go-ristretto — NTT module

  • Study: internal/ntt/*.go — forward/inverse NTT with clearly labelled CT and GS butterfly stages; readable butterfly index arithmetic.
  • Lessons: the butterfly index recurrence; how the "layers" of the NTT correspond to the divide-and-conquer structure; how twiddle factors are indexed per layer.
  • Pitfalls: different prime (q=2^255 - 19, not q=3329); zeta values do NOT transfer; use only for structural verification of butterfly staging logic in pqc-ntt.
  • Applied in: Phase 4 §4.2 (Cooley-Tukey CT butterfly layer structure verification, GS inverse NTT staging), §6 P9 (NTT ring algebra principle), Phase 2 §2.2 (NTT architecture concepts).

R9. Reference Keccak team code (keccak.noekeon.org)

  • Study: KeccakF-1600-reference.c (readable round function), KeccakF-1600-opt64.c (lane-interleaved optimised).
  • Lessons: the five Keccak-f steps (θ, ρ, π, χ, ι) with lane indexing A[x,y,z]; absorb/squeeze state transitions; multi-rate padding.
  • Pitfalls: the padding suffix byte for SHAKE (0x1F) vs SHA-3 (0x06) vs cSHAKE (0x04) is different; the opt64 version uses lane interleaving that requires explicit lane reordering — study the reference version first.
  • Applied in: Phase 5 §5.2 (Keccak-f[1600] five-step round function, θ/ρ/π/χ/ι structure), §6 P6 (hash-based domain separation principle), Phase 13 §13.2 (FIPS 202 SHAKE128/256 KAT verification).

S4 NIST SP 800-185 (Dec 2016) — SHA-3 Derived Functions cSHAKE128/256 (customized SHAKE); KMAC; TupleHash; domain-separation prefix encoding Context for extending pqc-hash; ML-KEM/DSA do not use cSHAKE directly but clarifies SHAKE vs cSHAKE boundary for pqc-hash API extensions

S9 RFC 8391 — XMSS (NIST SP 800-208) Stateful hash-based signature; Merkle-tree key management; state update requirements Contrast with ML-DSA: stateless Fiat-Shamir vs stateful Merkle; reinforces why ML-DSA's stateless design is operationally superior

6. Design Principles

P1. Constant-time on secret data

  • Mandate: no branch, memory-index, or early-exit depends on a secret value (secret key, noise polynomial, masking vector y, FO comparison result, m in decapsulation).
  • Mechanism: pqc-ct crate exports ct_eq(a: &[u8], b: &[u8]) -> Choice, ct_select<T: ConditionallySelectable>(a, b, c) -> T, zeroize_on_drop!(T) macro; all are built on subtle::Choice with black_box barriers.
  • Scope of public exceptions (see P7): matrix A expansion (expand_a) and uniform rejection sampling for public A coefficients may be variable-time.
  • Tradeoff: constant-time conditional 256-i32 copy costs 2–4× vs branching; constant-time ciphertext comparison (32 bytes) costs 4–6 ns extra per decapsulation; accepted unconditionally.
  • Verification: Phase 12 audit traces every function in pqc-kem + pqc-sign + pqc-ntt; dudect harness measures timing variance for 10^5 secret-value pairs; TVLA statistical test applied to cycle-count distributions.
  • Compilation flag: RUSTFLAGS="-C target-cpu=native" enables SIMD conditional moves; verify with objdump that no cmov degrades to jne.
  • Violation consequence: a single if ct_result == 0 { return K_good; } branch in decaps leaks 1 bit per query; 256 oracle queries determine the shared secret via binary search over the 256-bit comparison result.

P2. Specification-exact

  • Mandate: byte-for-byte match with FIPS 203/204 ACVP vectors for all 6 parameter sets on every operation.
  • What "exact" covers: public key byte layout, ciphertext byte layout, shared secret, signature byte layout; every compression rounding, every bit-packing order, every NTT domain ordering.
  • Non-negotiable constraint: FIPS 203 Appendix A specifies the NTT twiddle order and bit-packing direction; a "compatible" alternative encoding is not acceptable.
  • Tradeoff: specification exactness prohibits micro-optimisations that change output bytes (e.g., a different-but-equivalent bit-packing that is faster); algorithmic optimisations that preserve output (faster NTT butterfly order with the same output) are permitted.
  • Operational: pqc-kat runs before every merge; any byte-level divergence fails CI; the ACVP test case tcId and seed are logged on failure to enable exact reproduction.
  • Compatibility goal: a key generated by this crate can be used with the NIST reference C implementation, OpenSSL (once its ML-KEM lands), and any other FIPS 203 conforming library.
  • Violation consequence: an off-by-one in the bit-packing loop (shifting by d instead of d-1) produces public keys that decode to slightly different polynomial coefficients; the decoded t differs from the encoder's t by ±1 in some coefficients; decaps re-encrypts with wrong t and ct_cmp always mismatches, returning PRF(z,c) for every ciphertext including valid ones.

P3. CCA2 security via Fujisaki-Okamoto

  • Mandate: decaps NEVER returns an error; it always returns Ok(SharedSecret).
  • Algorithm: decaps(sk, c) → CPA decrypt m' → re-derive (K, r) = G(m', H(pk)) → re-encrypt c' = CPA_enc(pk, m'; r) → eq = ct_eq(c, c') : Choice → return ct_select(K, PRF(z, c), eq).
  • Secret path: ct_select is the only place the comparison result is used; neither the branch to PRF nor the return value may be conditioned on eq in a secret-dependent way beyond the ct_select itself.
  • Zeroization: m' is zeroized before decaps returns (P8); if m' leaks, the re-encryption key r leaks, which enables a distinguishing attack.
  • Tradeoff: decapsulation = 1 CPA-decrypt + 1 CPA-encrypt + 1 SHA3-512 + 1 32-byte ct_eq; ≈1.5× cost of decryption alone; accepted.
  • Operational: integration test verifies bitflipped ciphertext returns Ok(PRF(z,c)) with the correct 32-byte value; timing test confirms bitflipped and valid ciphertexts take statistically identical time.
  • Violation example: early exit if c.len() != CIPHERTEXT_BYTES { return Err(MalformedInput); } BEFORE the FO path is acceptable (length check is on public data); early exit if ct_eq != 1 { return Err(InvalidCiphertext); } after decryption is a security break.

P4. Rejection sampling preserves zero-knowledge

  • Mandate: the signing loop in pqc-sign::sign discards y and retries from fresh randomness whenever the response z = y + c*s1 fails the norm check or the low-bits check.
  • Rejection criteria (ML-DSA-65 example): ||z||_∞ ≥ γ1 - β = 2^17 - 196; ||LowBits(w - c*s2, 2γ2)||_∞ ≥ γ2 - β = 95232 - 196; popcount(h) > ω.
  • ZK argument: conditioned on acceptance, the distribution of z is uniform over the hypercube [-γ1+β, γ1-β]^{256*L}, statistically independent of s1; this follows from Lyubashevsky (2009, Pa-7).
  • Resampling requirement: y must be regenerated from a fresh SHAKE stream per iteration; reusing y across iterations correlates z_i values, enabling lattice-reduction recovery of s1.
  • Tradeoff: expected ~4 iterations for ML-DSA-65; tail probability of > 20 iterations is < 2^{-100}; practical latency variance is acceptable.
  • Timing consideration: loop iteration count is not secret (it is a property of the distribution, not the key); aggregate rejection counts may be logged without violating P10.
  • Violation example: checking ||z||_∞ < γ1 - β with a variable-time norm computation is safe (z is public after acceptance); NOT computing the norm at all and returning the first z would leak s1 via the linear relation z ≈ y (nearly uniform) + cs1 (biased) when ||cs1|| is large.

P5. The NTT is the multiplier, the schoolbook is the oracle

  • Mandate: all polynomial products in production code paths (keygen, encaps, decaps, sign, verify) use pqc-ntt::ntt_mul; pqc-poly::schoolbook_mul is #[cfg(test)] only.
  • NTT steps: ntt_forward(a): Poly<Normal> → Poly<Ntt>; ntt_forward(b): Poly<Normal> → Poly<Ntt>; ntt_pointwise_mul(â, b̂): (Poly<Ntt>, Poly<Ntt>) → Poly<Ntt>; ntt_inverse(ĉ): Poly<Ntt> → Poly<Normal>.
  • Complexity: NTT: O(256 * log(256)) = O(2048) multiplications; schoolbook: O(256²) = O(65536) multiplications; ~32× ratio.
  • Differential test coverage: prop_ntt_mul_eq_schoolbook with 10,000 random polynomial pairs (independent from Phase 3 tests); also tests the negacyclic property a*b in R_q = a*b mod (X^256+1).
  • Tradeoff: maintaining schoolbook_mul adds ~100 lines of code with zero production cost; the test oracle benefit is irreplaceable; removing it to save compile time is forbidden.
  • Violation example: accidentally enabling schoolbook_mul in non-test builds via #[cfg(not(target_arch = "...")) produces a 32× performance regression for any target that triggers the condition; ML-KEM-1024 keygen goes from ~120 µs to ~4 ms.

P6. Domain typing prevents misuse

  • Mandate: Poly is generic over Domain; ntt_mul has signature fn ntt_mul(a: Poly<Ntt>, b: Poly<Ntt>) -> Poly<Ntt>; poly_add has signature fn poly_add(a: Poly<Normal>, b: Poly<Normal>) -> Poly<Normal>.
  • Transitions: only ntt_forward: Poly<Normal> → Poly<Ntt> and ntt_inverse: Poly<Ntt> → Poly<Normal> change the domain tag; they are the single-entry-point gates.
  • PolyVec / PolyMat: PolyVec<D: Domain> = [Poly<D>; K]; the A matrix is always PolyMat<Ntt> after expansion; secret vectors s1/s2 are PolyVec<Normal> until NTT-transformed for multiplication.
  • Invariant link: I5 — mixing domains produces wrong arithmetic results; the type error is a compile-time gate preventing a runtime silent failure.
  • Cost: zero runtime overhead; phantom types are erased at compile time.
  • Tradeoff: adds two type parameters throughout; makes impl Mul for Poly non-trivially generic; requires careful trait bounds in pqc-ntt and pqc-kem.
  • Violation example (without domain types): calling ntt_mul(s, r) where s is a Normal-domain secret vector (fetched from key) and r is an Ntt-domain ephemeral (fetched from NTT output) compiles and runs; the product is neither s*r nor the pointwise product ŝ*r̂; the resulting ciphertext component u = A^T*r + e1 computed with wrong r is arithmetically invalid, decryption fails, and KAT fails at pqc-kat — but no error is raised during the computation.

P7. Public vs secret sampling differ

  • Mandate: pqc-sample::expand_a is explicitly variable-time; pqc-sample::sample_cbd and pqc-sample::sample_mask are explicitly constant-time; the distinction is documented in module-level //! comments and enforced by code review.
  • A expansion (variable-time): expand_a(rho: &Seed, i: u8, j: u8) -> Poly<Normal> uses SHAKE128 XOF; rejection-samples mod q by discarding bytes ≥ q (variable-time, safe because A is public).
  • CBD sampling (constant-time): sample_cbd(sigma: &Seed, nonce: u8, eta: u8) -> Poly<Normal> reads 64*eta bytes from PRF(sigma, nonce) and computes popcount(a[0..eta]) - popcount(a[eta..2*eta]) without any data-dependent branches; the output distribution is centred binomial Bη.
  • Mask sampling (constant-time): sample_mask(rho_prime: &Seed, nonce: u16, gamma1: u32) -> Poly<Normal> rejection-samples in constant time using a bit-masking strategy (not early-exit rejection) for the secret masking vector y.
  • Tradeoff: variable-time A expansion is 3–5× faster than a constant-time version; for keygen, A expansion dominates the runtime; the total keygen speedup from variable-time A is ≈40%.
  • Violation example: using the same early-exit rejection sampler for CBD noise as for A expansion causes the sampling time to correlate with the Hamming weight of the noise polynomial; each CBD coefficient ~B_2 has values {-2,-1,0,1,2} with known probabilities; the timing variance reveals partial information about the secret noise, reducing the effective noise entropy from η bits per coefficient.

P8. Secrets are zeroized

  • Mandate: every struct holding secret material implements zeroize::Zeroize via derive or manual implementation; Drop calls self.zeroize(); intermediate stack secrets (y, m, e) are wrapped in zeroize::Zeroizing<T>.
  • Covered secrets: KemSecretKey (s, pk_hash, z); SignSecretKey (ρ, K, tr, s1, s2, t0); masking vector y in sign loop; ephemeral message m in FO decapsulation; CPA randomness r.
  • Mechanism: zeroize crate uses core::ptr::write_volatile + a compiler fence (atomic::compiler_fence(SeqCst)) to prevent dead-code elimination of the wipe.
  • CI guard: cargo deny --features warn-on-missing-zeroize (or equivalent lint) flags structs with Secret-tagged fields that do not implement Zeroize.
  • Tradeoff: ~100 ns per key operation for zeroization; negligible vs the µs-scale lattice arithmetic; no observable user impact.
  • Stack vs heap: heap-allocated secrets (e.g., Box<KemSecretKey>) require Zeroizing<Box<...>> which zeroizes before dealloc; stack-allocated intermediates require let y: Zeroizing<PolyVec<Normal>> = ... wrapping.
  • Violation example: a sign function that creates let y: PolyVec<Normal> = sample_mask(...) on the stack and returns without zeroizing: after the function returns, Rust's default drop does NOT wipe the stack frame; the next function call on the same thread reuses the same stack addresses; a crash dump or a side-channel attacker with controlled heap layout can read y; combined with the output z, this directly recovers s1 = (z - y) / c.

P9. Fail closed on malformed input

  • Mandate: every public API entry point that accepts external bytes (ciphertext, signature, public key, secret key) validates length and coefficient range before any computation; validation failure returns Err(PqcError::*), never panics.
  • Validation layers: (1) length check: if bytes.len() != EXPECTED_BYTES { return Err(MalformedLength); }; (2) coefficient range: each decoded Zq value checked against [0, q); (3) norm check (signatures): ||z||_∞ < bound before accepting.
  • Error taxonomy: PqcError::MalformedLength, PqcError::CoefficientOutOfRange, PqcError::NormExceedsBound, PqcError::HintCountExceeded, PqcError::KatMismatch.
  • No partial acceptance: a public key with 99 valid bytes and 1 malformed byte is rejected entirely; partial state is not stored.
  • Fuzz gate: cargo-fuzz targets fuzz_decaps(ct_bytes), fuzz_verify(pk_bytes, msg, sig_bytes), fuzz_decode_poly(bytes) run 10^7 iterations with AddressSanitizer + UBSan in Phase 14; zero panics, zero OOB.
  • Tradeoff: validation adds 5–10 ns per call (a few comparisons + bounds checks); negligible.
  • Violation example: decode_polyvec(bytes: &[u8]) that performs coeffs[i] = u16::from_le_bytes([bytes[i*2], bytes[i*2+1]]) without checking bytes.len() >= 2*256*K panics with an index-out-of-bounds when passed a 1-byte ciphertext; this is a remotely-triggered crash on any system that calls pqc-kem::decaps with untrusted input.

P10. Observability without leakage

  • Mandate: telemetry is aggregate-only; no per-operation value is logged if it correlates with a specific secret; SigningStats and KemStats use atomic counters incremented unconditionally.
  • Permitted: total_sign_calls: AtomicU64, total_sign_retries: AtomicU64, total_encaps_calls: AtomicU64, total_decaps_calls: AtomicU64, total_decaps_rejections: AtomicU64; latency histograms (buckets only, not per-operation).
  • Forbidden in production: per-operation rejection count, per-operation signing latency tagged with key ID, any log entry containing a norm value or polynomial coefficient.
  • Debug builds: #[cfg(debug_assertions)] enables per-operation tracing with full intermediate values; this is disabled in release builds via cfg.
  • Operational: metrics module in pqc-core::telemetry; thread-safe via std::sync::atomic::AtomicU64; exposed via a get_stats() -> Stats function; never written to logs directly.
  • Tradeoff: aggregate statistics lose per-operation granularity needed for debugging individual failures; mitigated by debug-build tracing and the pqc-kat deterministic reproduction path.
  • Violation example: a production log entry info!("sign loop: {} retries for key {:?}", retry_count, &sk.rho) reveals (1) the signing key identity and (2) the rejection count for that specific invocation; an attacker collecting logs can identify keys with consistently high retry counts, indicating high-norm s1/s2, and apply targeted lattice reduction with fewer required signatures.

P11. Specification traceability — every algorithm step maps to a FIPS section

  • Mandate: every public function in pqc-kem and pqc-sign carries a doc-comment citing the FIPS 203 or FIPS 204 algorithm and section number from which it is derived (e.g., // FIPS 203 Algorithm 12, §6.2); every deviation from spec pseudocode is explicitly annotated as an optimization with a correctness argument.
  • Traceoff: adds roughly 2–5 lines of comment per function; negligible compile overhead; invaluable for audits.
  • Operational implication: ACVP failures can be traced to the specific algorithm step; the annotation serves as a checklist during code review.
  • Violation consequence: undocumented deviations from spec (e.g., a loop-index shift that happens to produce correct output on 95% of vectors) are not caught until a rare ACVP vector fails; root-cause analysis takes hours instead of seconds.

P12. No implicit cryptographic assumptions — every security argument is explicit

  • Mandate: every security-critical choice (e.g., q ≡ 1 mod 512 enabling full NTT, η bounding CBD noise for correct decryption, γ1 - β ensuring ZK in the rejection loop) is documented with a reference to the exact paper or FIPS section that derives the constraint; any code that changes a security parameter without updating the associated argument comment fails code review.
  • Tradeoff: documentation overhead is real but bounded; a parameter table in pqc-core/src/params.rs carries the proof-obligation for each value.
  • Violation consequence: silently choosing η=1 instead of η=2 for ML-KEM-768 reduces the noise margin, increasing the decryption failure rate from 2^-139 to a detectable level; without the explicit argument linking η to the failure-probability formula, this change may not be caught until statistical decryption failures appear in production.

7. System Invariants

The table below expands I1–I12. Column "Crate(s)" identifies the enforcement point. "Detection" covers the automated check. "Telemetry" lists metrics. "Recovery" covers the debugging and remediation path.

# Invariant Statement Why It Matters Crate(s) Detection Telemetry Recovery
I1 keygen/encaps/decaps/sign/verify outputs match FIPS 203/204 ACVP vectors byte-for-byte for all 6 parameter sets Byte divergence = interoperability failure + potential security break; "close" is wrong pqc-kat: one #[test] per (param-set × operation); assert_eq!(result, expected_bytes) CI cargo test -p pqc-kat; deterministic DRBG seeded from ACVP rng field; any failure halts merge kat_pass_count, kat_fail_count per (suite, param-set) Bisect to the crate/phase; compare intermediate arrays (pk, ct, sk, sig) against FIPS 203/204 Appendix A intermediate tables; add tracing to the Phase that diverged

I4 Decapsulating an invalid ciphertext returns Ok(PRF(z, c)), never Err(...) I5 inverse_ntt(forward_ntt(p)) == p; ntt_mul(a,b) == schoolbook_mul(a,b) in R_q Wrong NTT produces arithmetically wrong ciphertexts/signatures with no obvious error; only caught at KAT level pqc-ntt unit tests; prop_ntt_roundtrip and prop_ntt_mul_schoolbook in Phase 3 100,000 random poly roundtrips; 10,000 random multiplication pairs vs schoolbook; must pass before any higher crate builds None (correctness gate only) Failure: compare butterfly stages against reference C zeta table; verify ω = 17 for q=3329; check bit-reversal permutation order in twiddle precomputation; check Montgomery factor R = 2^16
I6 verify(pk, m, sign(sk, m)) == true for all honestly-generated keys and messages Self-generated signatures that fail = pqc-sign is broken; indicates arithmetic or packing error pqc-sign + pqc-encode integration test prop_sign_verify in Phase 11 1,000 keypairs × 3 param-sets × random 32-byte messages; also covered by I1 KATs sign_verify_fail_count (expected: 0) Failure: compare hint h between signer and verifier; trace HighBits/UseHint step-by-step against FIPS 204 sec. Algorithms 36–37; verify hint packing byte format
I7 Output signatures satisfy ||z||_∞ < γ1 - β; hint count ≤ ω; signing aborts and retries otherwise Out-of-bound z rejected by any conforming verifier; accepting also leaks directional info about s1 pqc-sign::sign (rejection loop + norm check); pqc-sign::verify (explicit bound checks) debug_assert!(z_inf_norm < GAMMA1_MINUS_BETA) before output; property test checks every produced signature; verify returns Err(NormExceedsBound) on violation sign_reject_loop_iterations_histogram (aggregate) Loop non-terminating: check η and γ1 - β constants in SignParams; verify PRNG seed expansion in pqc-sample::sample_mask correctly bounds y; inspect SHAKE256 nonce increment
I8 Every Zq after reduction lies in [0, q); no i32 intermediate overflows during butterfly accumulation Out-of-range coefficients corrupt NTT butterfly arithmetic silently in release builds pqc-core::Zq reduce post-condition; pqc-ntt lazy-reduction boundaries debug_assert!(0 <= v && v < Q) in Zq::reduce; property test prop_reduction_range for x ∈ [-q²,q²]; idempotence: reduce(reduce(x)) == reduce(x) None (debug assertions) Overflow: trace butterfly accumulation; count un-reduced additions before the lazy-reduction point; add barrett_reduce or montgomery_reduce one stage earlier in the butterfly loop
I9 decode(encode(x)) == x for lossless polyvecs; |decompress(compress(v,du)) - v|_∞ ≤ q/2^{du+1} for lossy Non-roundtripping encode corrupts ciphertext recovery (KEM) and packing (DSA); error exceeding FIPS bound causes decryption failures beyond the stated probability pqc-encode unit tests test_encode_roundtrip, test_compress_error_bound in Phase 6 prop_encode_roundtrip: 10,000 random polyvecs; exact equality (lossless); error-bound check (lossy compress) None Failure: check bit-packing endianness (little-endian within each d-bit word); check Compress_q rounding (round-to-nearest, not floor) to satisfy the FIPS-specified error bound
I10 Any malformed ciphertext/signature/key byte sequence returns Err(PqcError::*) without panic, OOB, or secret leak Remote panic = DoS; OOB = memory unsafety; secret leak = key recovery pqc-encode decode validators; pqc-kem, pqc-sign, pqc-cli entry-point guards; Phase 14 fuzz cargo-fuzz targets fuzz_decaps, fuzz_verify, fuzz_decode_poly, fuzz_decode_sig: ≥10^7 iterations with ASan + UBSan; zero panics required malformed_input_reject_count (aggregate) Panic in fuzz: add missing length guard before first array index; add coefficient range check in decode_poly; add hint-count range check in decode_signature; rerun fuzz to confirm
I11 Debug/Display on any secret-holding type (KemSecretKey, SignSecretKey, masking y, SharedSecret) never prints raw secret bytes; only a redacted marker is emitted Auto-derived Debug is a common accidental-logging leak vector (error paths, panic messages, tracing spans that {:?}-format a struct) pqc-core, pqc-kem, pqc-sign: manual Debug impl on every secret-holding type Unit test asserts format!("{:?}", secret) never contains the true byte sequence; clippy::missing_debug_implementations combined with a custom lint denying #[derive(Debug)] on secret types secret_debug_format_total (should be 0 outside the redacted-marker test) Grep for #[derive(Debug)] on secret types in CI; replace with manual impl printing <redacted>
I12 Every public API operation validates its KemParams/SignParams argument against the caller's key/ciphertext/signature length before use; a parameter-set mismatch returns Err(ParamMismatch), never executes silently under the wrong parameter set Accepting e.g. an ML-DSA-87 signature under ML-DSA-44 constants would silently corrupt norm-bound checks (T8) pqc-kem, pqc-sign API boundary: length/tag assert before any arithmetic Unit test per param-set pairing: cross-param-set input must return Err(ParamMismatch), never a wrong-but-plausible result param_mismatch_reject_total (aggregate) Audit API boundary asserts; add the missing length/tag check; add a regression fixture for the missed pairing

Invariant / Principle Cross-Reference

The table below identifies which principle(s) govern each invariant and which phases are responsible for establishing and maintaining it.

Invariant Governing Principles Established In Maintained In Primary Risk If Violated
I1 (KAT exact) P2 Phases 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 13 pqc-kat CI gate Interoperability failure; potential security break
I2 (constant-time) P1, P7 Phase 12 (hardening) pqc-ct, every crate using secrets CCA2 break via timing oracle
I3 (KEM correctness) P2, P3 Phase 7 (CPA PKE) pqc-kem, pqc-encode (compress/decomp) Session key mismatch; protocol failure
I4 (implicit rejection) P3, P1 Phase 8 (FO KEM) pqc-kem::decaps only CCA2 break; full session key recovery
I5 (NTT correctness) P5, P6 Phase 3 (NTT) pqc-ntt differential tests Silent wrong ciphertext/signature
I6 (sig correctness) P2, P4 Phase 10–11 (sign/verify) pqc-sign, pqc-encode Unusable signature implementation
I7 (norm bounds) P4, P2 Phase 10 (signing loop) pqc-sign::sign + verify Security break (leak s1) or interoperability rejection
I8 (reduction range) P2, P5 Phase 1 (Zq) pqc-core::Zq, pqc-ntt Silent corruption of arithmetic
I9 (encoding roundtrip) P2, P9 Phase 6 (encode) pqc-encode Decryption failure; packing corruption
I10 (malformed input) P9, P1 Phase 6, 7, 8, 11, 14 pqc-encode, pqc-kem, pqc-sign DoS via panic; potential secret leak
I11 (no secret in Debug) P10, P8 Phase 1 (key types), Phase 12 (audit) pqc-core, pqc-kem, pqc-sign Accidental secret exposure via logs/panics
I12 (param-set match enforced) P2, P9 Phase 7 (KEM API), Phase 9 (DSA API) pqc-kem, pqc-sign API boundary Silent cross-param-set corruption (T8)

Invariant Failure Taxonomy

Failure Class Symptomatic Invariant(s) First Observed Cause Category Detection Latency
Byte-level divergence from FIPS vectors I1 pqc-kat CI Encoding endianness; wrong domain-separation byte; off-by-one in bit-packing Immediate (CI on merge)
Correctness failure (decaps returns wrong ss) I3, I9 prop_kem_correctness Compress/decompress rounding error; wrong du/dv constants Immediate (unit test)
Non-implicit-rejection (Err returned) I4 Bitflip property test Premature early-return in decaps; missing ct_select Immediate (integration test)
NTT wrong product I5 prop_ntt_mul_schoolbook Wrong zeta table; wrong butterfly ordering; wrong Montgomery factor Phase 3 gate
Signature self-verification failure I6 prop_sign_verify HighBits/UseHint mismatch; hint packing error Phase 11 gate
Norm bound violation I7 Phase 10 debug_assert Wrong SignParams constants; sample_mask out-of-range Debug build
Coefficient range violation I8 Phase 1 prop test Lazy reduction not applied at correct butterfly stage Phase 1 gate
Timing variance detected I2 dudect harness Secret-dependent branch; secret-indexed table Phase 12 gate
Panic on malformed input I10 cargo-fuzz Missing length guard; missing coefficient range check Nightly fuzz

Required Reading Priority Order

For a new contributor, the recommended reading sequence to implement the phases correctly:

  1. FIPS 203 sec. 2 (ML-KEM algorithms) + FIPS 204 sec. 2 (ML-DSA algorithms) — normative spec first.
  2. Pa-3 (Kyber submission) sec. 2–3; Pa-4 (Dilithium submission) sec. 2–3 — design rationale.
  3. HAC sec. 14.3 (Montgomery) + Pa-8 (Longa/Naehrig NTT) — arithmetic backbone for Phases 1–3.
  4. FIPS 202 + B3 Ch. 12 — pqc-hash correctness before Phases 4–5 can be tested.
  5. Pa-5 + Pa-6 (FO transform) — before implementing Phase 8.
  6. Pa-7 (Fiat-Shamir with aborts) — before implementing Phase 10.
  7. B4 Ch. 5 + Ch. 17 + Pa-11 + Pa-12 (side channels) — before Phase 12 hardening.
  8. R7 (ACVP vectors) — before Phase 13 KAT harness.

8. Domain Model

8.1 Core Type Hierarchy

Seed (32 B)
  └─ rho (public matrix seed), sigma (noise seed), K (KEM rejection seed),
     rho' (sign key seed), context-dependent naming per FIPS 203/204

Zq (i32, range [0, q))
  └─ ML-KEM: q = 3329; ML-DSA: q = 8380417

Poly { coeffs: [Zq; 256] } tagged Domain { Normal | Ntt }
  └─ PolyVec = [Poly; K]   (rank-K column vector)
  └─ PolyMat = [[Poly; L]; K]  (K×L matrix)

KemParams { k: usize, eta1: usize, eta2: usize, du: usize, dv: usize }
SignParams { k: usize, l: usize, eta: usize, beta: i32, gamma1: i32,
             gamma2: i32, tau: usize, lambda: usize, omega: usize }
Entity Representation Invariants
Zq i32 0 <= v < q; intermediate mul may exceed i32 → use i64 accumulator, reduce immediately
Poly (Normal) [i32; 256] coeffs in (-q, q) or [0,q) (context-dependent) Domain tag Normal; schoolbook/NTT ops start here
Poly (NTT) [i32; 256] in NTT domain Domain tag Ntt; pointwise mul only; must inv_ntt before compress/encode
PolyVec [Poly; K] All polys share same Domain; ML-KEM K ∈ {2,3,4}
PolyMat [[Poly; K]; L] or [[Poly; L]; K] Expanded from rho via XOF; always in Ntt domain post-expansion
KemPublicKey `encode(t1
KemSecretKey `encode(s)
Ciphertext encode(u, v) compressed bytes du/dv bits per coeff per parameter set
SharedSecret [u8; 32] KEM output K; indistinguishable from random if ciphertext tampered
SignPublicKey `rho
SignSecretKey `rho
Signature `c_tilde
Challenge Poly with exactly tau non-zero ±1 coefficients Sparse; generated by H(tr, msg, w1)
Hint [[bool; 256]; K] bit array At most omega total 1-bits; encodes carry corrections
