State during decaps: dk (2400 B) + m' (32 B) + (K, r) from G (64 B) + c' reconstruction (1088 B) + intermediate polys (3 × 256 × 4 B = 3072 B). Peak stack: 2400 + 32 + 64 + 1088 + 3072 ≈ 6656 bytes — fits within a 16 KB embedded stack.

8. NTT twiddle table size. Forward NTT for q=3329: 128 Montgomery-encoded zetas × 4 bytes each = 512 bytes (fits in L1 cache; 8 KB L1 on Cortex-A72). For q=8380417 (ML-DSA): 256 zetas × 4 bytes = 1024 bytes — also L1-resident.

9. ACVP KAT vector file size. Each ML-KEM vector: seed (48 B hex) + ek (1184 B hex) + dk (2400 B hex) + m (32 B) + c (1088 B) + K (32 B) ≈ 4784 B per vector. Standard ACVP test group: 100 vectors per parameter set × 3 KEM + 3 DSA sets = 600 total vectors. Total JSON size: 600 × 4784 B × 2 (hex encoding overhead) ≈ 5.7 MB of JSON files — negligible in CI.

10. Constant-time comparison cost. ct_eq_bytes for 32-byte shared secret: XOR 32 bytes, OR-reduce, mask — approximately 32 × 2 = 64 instructions. At 1.5 GHz: 64 / 1.5 × 10^9 ≈ 43 ns per comparison — adds 0.008 % overhead to a 500 µs decaps.

11. Signing key size (ML-DSA-65). From FIPS 204 Table 1: |sk| = 32 + 32 + 64 + 32(l+k)·eta_bits where eta=4, l=5, k=6. = 32 (ρ) + 32 (K) + 64 (tr) + 32×5×3 + 32×6×3 = 32 + 32 + 64 + 480 + 576 = 4032 bytes (matches FIPS 204: 4032 bytes).

Hard Questions

Each question names the resolving § and requires synthesis of at least two sections to answer.

HQ-01. Why does FO decapsulation return Ok(PRF(z,c)) on a bad ciphertext rather than Err(InvalidCiphertext), and what is the exact attack if you return an error instead? Resolves §6 P3 + §8 (Phase 8). An error return tells an adversary whether their tampered ciphertext passed the re-encryption check; 256 binary oracle queries binary-search the 256-bit comparison to recover the shared secret K. PRF(z,c) is pseudorandom and unconditionally hides the oracle result.

HQ-02. FIPS 203 Appendix A specifies NTT zeta ordering as bit-reversed. What precisely does bit-reversal mean for a 128-element table, and what would happen if you used natural order? Resolves Phase 3 §3.2 + Phase 4 §4.2. Bit-reversal reorders the primitive 256th root's powers so each butterfly reads its twiddle from position bitrev(layer_index). Natural order would compute the correct product for layer 0 but produce wrong twiddles for all subsequent layers, causing the inverse NTT to fail to cancel the forward NTT; schoolbook-NTT output comparison (I5) would catch this in Phase 4.

HQ-03. Can you reuse the masking vector y across two signing attempts? Why or why not? Resolves §6 P4 + Phase 8 §8.2 (Pa-7). If y is reused across two accepted signatures (z1 = y + c1·s1, z2 = y + c2·s1), then z1 − z2 = (c1 − c2)·s1. Since c1 and c2 are known, this is a linear equation in s1; a lattice-reduction attack on the resulting short-vector instance recovers s1 from a small number of such pairs.

HQ-04. ML-KEM-512 targets "Category 1" (AES-128 equivalent). What does that mean concretely, and why does it NOT mean the key is 128 bits? Resolves §4 System Constraints + §6 P8 (Pa-1, Pa-2). Category 1 means the best known quantum attack requires at least as many quantum gates as exhaustive search on AES-128. The RLWE problem dimension (n=256, k=2) determines the concrete hardness; the shared secret is 32 bytes (256 bits) of output, but the security level is measured against the hardest known lattice attack (BKZ with quantum subroutines), not key size.

HQ-05. What is the decryption failure rate δ for ML-KEM-768, and why does it matter for long-term key pairs? Resolves §4 System Constraints + Threat Model (Pa-2). δ ≈ 2^{-164} for ML-KEM-768 (FIPS 203 sec. 3.3). For a key pair used in 10^9 encapsulations (CDN-scale), the probability of any failure is 10^9 × 2^{−164} ≈ 0 for practical purposes. A non-negligible δ would allow an attacker to submit ciphertexts crafted near the decryption boundary and use the failure signal as an oracle — the tight δ prevents this.

HQ-06. What exactly does "implicit rejection" mean, and how does it differ from "explicit rejection"? Resolves §6 P3 + Phase 7 §7.2 (S5). Implicit rejection: on FO mismatch, return PRF(z, c) (a pseudorandom value determined only by the stored z and the ciphertext, not by the message). Explicit rejection: return an error or a distinguished constant. Implicit is stronger because the adversary cannot determine from the output whether decapsulation succeeded; explicit leaks a binary bit per query.

HQ-07. Why does pqc-ntt need both a Montgomery reduction AND a Barrett reduction? Can you use only one? Resolves Phase 3 §3.2 + §6 P5. Montgomery reduction is optimized for the inner NTT butterfly loop (multiply by a precomputed constant — the Montgomery-encoded zeta); it avoids division but requires the input to be in Montgomery form. Barrett reduction is used for general one-off reductions of arbitrary products (e.g., after accumulating coefficients in an inner product). Using Montgomery everywhere would require entering/exiting Montgomery form for every non-NTT operation; using Barrett everywhere is slower in the NTT inner loop.

HQ-08. How does the Domain phantom tag eliminate an entire class of NTT correctness bugs, and what is the performance cost? Resolves ADR-10 + Phase 2 §2.2. The tag adds 1 byte to the Poly struct (or zero bytes if const-generic), but the primary benefit is that poly_add(a: Poly<Normal>, b: Poly<Ntt>) becomes a type error at compile time. The runtime cost is zero in release mode (the field is stripped or inlined); the debug-mode assert costs one conditional check per operation.

HQ-09. If you replace SHAKE128 with AES-256-CTR as the XOF for matrix A expansion, what changes and what breaks? Resolves §6 P6 + Phase 5 §5.2 (Pa-8). AES-256-CTR is a valid PRF but it is not a standardized ML-KEM XOF; FIPS 203 mandates SHAKE128 for expand_A. Switching breaks FIPS 203 ACVP KAT vectors immediately (byte-level mismatch). Additionally, AES hardware acceleration varies more across embedded targets than SHAKE; on constrained targets without AES-NI, CTR is slower than SHAKE.

HQ-10. The signing loop iterates until norm bounds pass. Can an adversary influence how many iterations occur, and does that leak anything? Resolves §6 P4 + Phase 8 §8.2 + Threat Model (T5). The number of iterations is a function of the random y draws and the challenge c, both derived from public values (message, seed). The iteration count is variable-time (ADR-11 allows variable-time loop count) because it leaks only statistical information about the public y/c relationship — it does not directly reveal s1. An adversary observing timing can count iterations but cannot invert the one-way function from iteration count to s1.

HQ-11. What is the "hint" vector in ML-DSA, why is it needed, and what happens if MakeHint produces more than ω hints? Resolves Phase 9 §9.2 + Phase 10 §10.2 (Pa-6). The hint vector h encodes carry-correction bits: the verifier has t1 (the high-order bits of t) but needs to reconstruct HighBits(w − c·s2 + c·t0, 2γ2). UseHint corrects for the rounding done during key generation. If popcount(h) > ω, the signature exceeds the hint budget — the signing loop discards this y sample and retries. Without this bound, an adversary could craft high-hint signatures that leak information about t0 or s2.

HQ-12. How does the KAT architecture ensure byte-exact FIPS 203/204 compliance rather than just functional correctness? Resolves Phase 13 §13.2 + §4 System Constraints (S7). Functional correctness (keygen → encaps → decaps → matching key) is necessary but not sufficient: the shared secret byte string must match the ACVP-specified expected output for a given 48-byte seed. pqc-kat feeds the seed into the same deterministic DRBG specified in ACVP, calls the API, and compares every output byte. A "functionally correct" but byte-incompatible implementation would interoperate with nothing in the PQC ecosystem.

HQ-13. Why does ML-DSA use a 48-byte (384-bit) signing key seed rather than a 32-byte seed? Resolves Phase 9 §9.2 + §6 P8. The seed expands via SHAKE-256 to (ρ, ρ', K), three 32-byte values: ρ for A expansion, ρ' for s1/s2 expansion, K for signing randomization. A 32-byte seed would allow only two 256-bit derivations, forcing key reuse or a second derivation step. FIPS 204 sec. 5.1 specifies the 32+32+32 derivation explicitly; the 48-byte external representation in some contexts is a different representation of the same seed material.

HQ-14. What is the difference between a constant-time function and a constant-time implementation, and why does the difference matter for Rust on different targets? Resolves §6 P1 + Phase 12 §12.2 (Pa-14). A function is constant-time if its execution time is independent of secret inputs at the mathematical level. A constant-time implementation is one where the compiled machine code on a specific target CPU exhibits no secret-dependent timing. Rust can compile the same source to a cmov (CT) on x86-64 but to a beq/bne branch (non-CT) on some RISC-V targets without hardware conditional-move. dudect must be run on the deployment target, not just x86-64.

HQ-15. What would it cost to add masking (t-order) to pqc-kem's decapsulation to defend against higher-order power analysis, and is it worth it? Resolves Phase 12 §12.2 + Phase 14 §14.2 (Pa-13). First-order masking doubles the number of polynomial operations (all operate on shares); second-order masking quadruples it. For ML-KEM-768 decaps (≈300 µs baseline), t=1 masking adds ≈300 µs overhead — 600 µs total. This exceeds the §4 target of 500 µs. Masking is worth it only in a physical-access threat model (smart card, HSM) where power traces are observable; in a pure network threat model (no physical access), timing-only CT is sufficient.

HQ-16. If the NTT pointwise multiplication step has a bug that affects only one of the 128 coefficient positions, will the KAT catch it? Resolves Phase 4 §4.2 + Phase 13 §13.2. Yes. The KAT vectors are derived from known-good implementations where every coefficient of the output polynomial is fixed. A bug affecting position k produces a wrong coefficient in the output ciphertext or shared secret; the KAT byte comparison reports a mismatch on the first byte position affected. The schoolbook reference oracle (I5) in Phase 4 would also catch this independently before KAT.

HQ-17. What are the exact conditions under which Barrett reduction produces output outside [0, q), and how does pqc-ntt handle this? Resolves Phase 3 §3.2 + I6 (invariant on coefficient range). Barrett reduction approximates floor(a/q) using a precomputed magic number; the approximation error is ±1 for inputs in the allowed range. If the input exceeds 2^{2k}/q (the overflow threshold for the chosen k), the output can exceed q by 1. pqc-ntt applies a final conditional subtract (if coeff >= q { coeff -= q }) implemented as a constant-time conditional move to bring the result into [0, q).

HQ-18. How does the pqc-cli kat subcommand differ from the pqc-kat library, and what would break if they were merged? Resolves Phase 13 §13.2 + Phase 14 §14.2. pqc-kat is a no_std + optional alloc library crate with no I/O; it takes pre-loaded vectors and returns pass/fail results. pqc-cli is a binary with std, file I/O, and user-facing formatting. Merging them would pull std (and clap, and file I/O) into the core library, breaking the no_std constraint that makes pqc-kat usable in embedded test harnesses. The separation follows ADR-12.

Alternative Architectures

Each alternative is fundamentally different from the primary design. For each: ASCII sketch of the conceptual structure, regime where it wins, a real system that chose it, and why it is not the primary choice here.

Alt-1: Hash-Based Signatures (SLH-DSA / SPHINCS+, FIPS 205)

SLH-DSA structure:
  Signing key ──► SK.seed ──SHAKE──► FORS trees + HT layers
                                         │
                  PK = H(FORS.pk ‖ HT.pk)│
                                         ▼
  Sign: choose FORS leaf idx → FORS auth path + FORS sig
        + HT sig (d-layer hash-based Merkle path)
  Verify: recompute FORS root + HT root; check PK

Regime where it wins: maximum confidence in post-quantum security when even lattice hardness is doubted; defense against unknown quantum attacks on algebraic structure; regulatory mandates that prohibit lattice-based algorithms. Real system: SPHINCS+ (Round-3 alternate NIST finalist), now standardized as FIPS 205 SLH-DSA. Why not primary: signatures are 7856 – 49856 bytes (vs ML-DSA-65's 3309 bytes); signing is 10–100× slower due to many hash evaluations; the stateless design requires a huge one-time hash tree. For the KEM use case, hash-based KEM (BIKE, Classic McEliece) has similar drawbacks. ML-DSA at 3309 bytes is a far more practical choice for TLS and code-signing workloads.

Alt-2: Code-Based Cryptography (Classic McEliece)

Classic McEliece structure:
  Public key: n×k binary matrix G (over GF(2))
  Encrypt: m → c = m·G + e  (e = random weight-t error vector)
  Decrypt: Berlekamp-Massey error correction
           → unique m if weight(e) ≤ t

Regime where it wins: highest confidence against quantum attacks among all NIST finalists (McEliece's 50-year security track record); no known structural attacks even with quantum computers; preferred in ultra-high-assurance contexts (nuclear command-and-control, long-lived government communications where 50-year security is required). Real system: Classic McEliece KEM (NIST Round 4 finalist, not yet standardized); liboqs includes McEliece implementations. Why not primary: public keys are 261 KB – 1 MB depending on parameter set; this is impractical for TLS (client hello size limit), code signing, or embedded use; the primary blueprint targets TLS-compatible key sizes (≤ 2 KB public key).

Alt-3: Plain-LWE (Ring-LWE, NTRU) without Module Structure

Ring-LWE structure (degree-n ring, not module):
  a ← R_q[x]/(x^n + 1), s, e ← χ_σ
  b = a·s + e  (product in ring, not matrix-vector)
  KEM encaps: u = a·r + e1, v = b·r + e2 + round(q/2)m

Regime where it wins: smaller key/ciphertext sizes for equal security level when module rank k=1 is sufficient (pure Ring-LWE at n=1024 achieves 256-bit security with smaller keys than ML-KEM-1024 at n=256, k=4); better suited for very constrained MCUs where memory for a k×k matrix is prohibitive. Real system: NTRU (original), NewHope (deprecated NIST candidate, used by Google in an early TLS experiment). Why not primary: FIPS 203 standardizes Module-LWE (ML-KEM), not Ring-LWE; using plain Ring-LWE would produce a non-FIPS-compliant implementation. Module structure also provides algebraic flexibility: the module rank k can be varied to tune security without changing the polynomial degree n=256.

Alt-4: Isogeny-Based Cryptography (SIDH/SIKE — historical only)

SIDH structure (historical, broken 2022):
  E: elliptic curve over GF(p^2)
  Alice: choose secret isogeny φ_A of degree l_A^{e_A}
  Bob:   choose secret isogeny φ_B of degree l_B^{e_B}
  Shared: j-invariant of E / (φ_A ∘ φ_B)

Regime where it wins: historically offered the smallest public keys (~330 bytes for SIKEp434) among all PQC candidates; interesting for extremely bandwidth-constrained protocols. Real system: SIKE (NIST Round-3 alternate finalist); SIKEp434 was the most studied variant. Why not primary: SIKE was completely broken in July 2022 by Castryck-Decru (an efficient classical polynomial-time attack using the torsion-point information leakage); the Castryck-Decru attack is based on a mathematical insight about auxiliary points that SIDH requires for efficiency. No current isogeny-based KEM scheme has survived similar scrutiny at usable parameter sizes; the field is active (CSIDH, SQIsign) but not yet standardized. Including isogeny-based schemes would violate P12 (no implicit cryptographic assumptions) given the 2022 break.

Deferred Features Register

Features consciously excluded from the current 14-phase scope. Each entry carries an anchor, the reason for deferral, and the re-evaluation trigger.

ID Feature Deferred Reason Re-evaluation Trigger
DF-01 SLH-DSA / SPHINCS+ (FIPS 205) Out of scope for this blueprint; requires a separate pqc-slh crate with completely different tree-hashing structure; no shared code with ML-KEM/ML-DSA NTT path FIPS 205 final publication; customer contract requiring hash-based backup signature
DF-02 Falcon (NTRU-Lattice signature) Falcon requires Gaussian sampling over NTRU lattices and floating-point arithmetic (or fixed-point emulation); fundamentally different from Fiat-Shamir with aborts; separate crate with distinct security review NIST standardization of Falcon (expected as FIPS 206); embedded target requiring smaller signatures than ML-DSA
DF-03 AVX2 / AVX-512 SIMD NTT Requires inline assembly or C FFI; adds unsafe surface; portability fragility; benchmark data shows scalar NTT already meets the 1 µs/poly target on x86-64 NTT benchmark regression (>1.5 µs/poly) on target hardware; high-throughput server use-case with >100K encaps/s requirement
DF-04 AArch64 NEON NTT Same rationale as DF-03; scalar meets targets on Cortex-A72; NEON gains ~2× but at unsafe+assembly cost Embedded deployment on Cortex-A55/A72 where power budget makes throughput-per-watt critical
DF-05 t-order masking (power-analysis resistance) Doubles/quadruples polynomial operation count; exceeds 500 µs decaps target for t=1; no current threat model requires physical access Smart card / HSM deployment contract; physical-access threat model; EAL4+ evaluation
DF-06 Formal CT verification (ct-verif / binsec) Rust toolchain integration immature; no first-class binsec support; FIPS 140-3 lab test is the practical gate Government contract requiring formal proof of constant-time (FIPS 140-3 Level 3+)
DF-07 WASM target and wasm-pack integration WASM's linear memory dump risk requires audit of zeroize effectiveness under WASM engine; no current use-case requires in-browser PQC Browser-based KEM (e.g., WebCrypto PQC API adoption); wasm-pack ecosystem maturity
DF-08 TLS 1.3 hybrid KEM integration (X25519MLKEM768) Requires TLS stack integration (rustls or OpenSSL binding); out of scope for a standalone PQC library; X25519 dependency adds classical EC code Customer requiring drop-in rustls integration; RFC for X25519MLKEM768 becomes IETF Standard
DF-09 Stateful hash-based KEM (XMSS, LMS) Stateful schemes require persistent state management (sequence counter); introduces database dependency; incompatible with no_alloc no-state design Key-encapsulation use-cases requiring 50+ year security guarantee; NIST SP 800-208 explicit mandate
DF-10 Classic McEliece KEM 261 KB – 1 MB public keys; impractical for TLS; fundamentally different code-based algebra; separate crate High-assurance government communication requiring 50-year quantum-resistant guarantee with no algebraic structure assumptions
DF-11 ACVP automated submission pipeline Requires ACVP server account and live network access; CI is offline; KAT vectors are checked-in for determinism NIST FIPS validation lab engagement; FIPS 140-3 submission
DF-12 Persistent HSM key storage (PKCS#11 interface) PKCS#11 requires OS-level daemon, file I/O, or hardware driver; beyond no_std crate scope Deployment in an HSM (Thales, SafeNet) where ML-KEM must interoperate with PKCS#11 middleware
DF-13 Hardware-accelerated SHA3 (VAES / SHA-NI-equivalent intrinsics) Requires target-specific intrinsics or inline assembly; adds unsafe surface; scalar Keccak-f[1600] already meets the SHAKE128 throughput target (Performance Engineering M9) Production profiling identifies SHA3/SHAKE as the dominant bottleneck; stable Rust intrinsics for Keccak-targeted vector instructions become available

Glossary

Alphabetical. Each term defined to its implementation-relevant meaning in this codebase.

Term Definition
ACVP Automated Cryptographic Validation Protocol — NIST framework supplying the JSON-encoded KAT vectors used in pqc-kat.
Barrett reduction Integer division by a fixed modulus via precomputed reciprocal multiply and shift; avoids hardware divide; used for ML-DSA's q=8380417.
CBD / Centered Binomial Distribution CBD_eta: sample eta pairs of bits, return difference; exact distribution for secret/noise poly coefficients in ML-KEM; constant-time from PRF output.
CCA2 security Chosen-ciphertext attack (adaptive) — an adversary who can query a decryption oracle on any ciphertext except the challenge cannot distinguish shared secrets; achieved here by the Fujisaki-Okamoto transform.
Challenge polynomial (c) Sparse polynomial with exactly tau coefficients in {+1, -1} and the rest 0; derived by hashing (tr, msg, w1) in ML-DSA signing; defines the Fiat-Shamir binding.
CPA security Chosen-plaintext attack — the weaker base security of the Module-LWE PKE before the FO transform lifts it to CCA2.
Compression / Decompress_q Lossy rounding: Compress_q(x,d) = round(2^d/q * x) mod 2^d; Decompress_q reverses; used on KEM ciphertext components (u,v) to reduce bandwidth; introduces bounded noise.
Decapsulation failure Event where decaps(sk, encaps(pk)) ≠ intended shared secret; negligible probability bounded by the module-LWE noise parameters; analyzed per parameter set in FIPS 203.
Domain tag enum Domain { Normal, Ntt } — a Poly carries its domain; the type system prevents multiplying a Normal poly against an NTT poly without explicit conversion.
Fiat-Shamir with aborts Lyubashevsky's signature paradigm: commit, receive challenge, respond with z = y + c·s1; abort and retry if z or residual exceeds the norm bound, to prevent secret leakage via response distribution.
FIPS 203 NIST Federal Information Processing Standard 203 — the normative ML-KEM specification; defines parameter sets 512/768/1024, all encodings, and the KAT vectors.
FIPS 204 NIST Federal Information Processing Standard 204 — the normative ML-DSA specification; defines parameter sets 44/65/87, all encodings, signing/verification algorithms, and the KAT vectors.
Fujisaki-Okamoto (FO) transform Converts a CPA-secure PKE into a CCA2-secure KEM: re-encrypt on decapsulation and constant-time compare; mismatch returns PRF(z,c) (implicit rejection).
G (hash function) SHA3-512 as defined in FIPS 203; used to derive (K,r) from the message and public-key hash during KEM encapsulation.
H (hash function) SHA3-256 as defined in FIPS 203/204; used to hash the public key into the KEM secret key (H(pk)) and in signature tr.
Hint (h) Carry-correction bits in ML-DSA signatures; allow verifier to reconstruct w1' from A·z - c·t1·2^d without access to the secret t0.
High bits / Low bits HighBits(r, alpha): upper portion of r mod q used in ML-DSA commitment w1; LowBits: remainder; decomposition defined in FIPS 204 Algorithm 9/10.
Implicit rejection On decapsulation failure the KEM returns PRF(z, c) instead of an error; the caller cannot distinguish valid from invalid ciphertext by the returned value or timing.
KAT (Known-Answer Test) A deterministic test vector pairing a seed/input with an exact expected output; pqc-kat asserts byte-exact agreement with FIPS 203/204 ACVP vectors for every parameter set.
Keccak-f[1600] The underlying permutation of SHA-3 and SHAKE; a 1600-bit state updated via 24 rounds of theta/rho/pi/chi/iota steps; implemented from scratch in pqc-hash.
KEM (Key Encapsulation Mechanism) A public-key primitive outputting a shared secret and a ciphertext; the shared secret is never transmitted; here constructed via Module-LWE PKE + FO transform.
Lattice / Module lattice A discrete additive subgroup of R^n; Module-LWE and Module-SIS are hard problems over module lattices of rank k over a polynomial ring; hardness is quantum-resistant.
LWE (Learning With Errors) Computational problem: given A, b = A·s + e (e small noise), find s; believed hard classically and quantumly; the security foundation of ML-KEM.
ML-DSA Module-Lattice-Based Digital Signature Algorithm — the FIPS 204 standard; concrete parameter sets replace the submission-era "Dilithium" names.
ML-KEM Module-Lattice-Based Key-Encapsulation Mechanism — the FIPS 203 standard; concrete parameter sets replace the submission-era "Kyber" names.
Module-LWE LWE over a module of rank k over R_q = Z_q[X]/(X^256+1); the matrix A is k×k (KEM) or k×l (DSA); larger k raises security at cost of key size.
Module-SIS Short Integer Solution problem over modules; the computational assumption underlying ML-DSA binding.
Montgomery reduction Multiplication mod q using a precomputed R=2^16 (ML-KEM) or R=2^23 (ML-DSA) such that Montgomery_reduce(a·b) = a·b·R^{-1} mod q; avoids division; used in NTT butterflies.
Negacyclic convolution Polynomial multiplication modulo X^256+1 (not X^256-1); the NTT exploits the negacyclic structure via 2n-th roots of unity.
Norm bound ML-DSA: signing accepts z only when
NTT (Number-Theoretic Transform) Discrete Fourier transform over Z_q using q-th roots of unity; converts polynomial multiplication to pointwise multiplication in O(n log n); pqc-ntt implements Cooley-Tukey forward and Gentleman-Sande inverse.
Parameter set A named tuple of cryptographic constants (k, l, eta, du, dv, gamma1, gamma2, tau, …) defining a security level; ML-KEM-512/768/1024 and ML-DSA-44/65/87 are the six sets.
Pointwise multiplication Element-wise multiply of two NTT-domain PolyVecs; equivalent to polynomial multiplication in R_q after inverse NTT.
PRF Pseudo-random function — instantiated as SHAKE256 in FIPS 203/204; used to generate CBD noise from a seed and a counter byte.
Rejection sampling In ML-KEM: discard uniform XOF output bytes ≥ 19·q mod q (keeps distribution uniform mod q) to generate matrix A. In ML-DSA: discard a candidate signature when norm bounds are exceeded.
rho 32-byte public seed embedded in public keys; deterministically expands via XOF to the full public matrix A; not secret.
R_q The quotient ring Z_q[X]/(X^256+1); elements are degree-255 polynomials with coefficients in Z_q; the algebraic structure underlying both ML-KEM and ML-DSA.
Seed (rho / sigma / K) 32-byte octet strings fed to SHAKE for deterministic expansion; rho generates A (public), sigma generates s1/s2/e (secret), K is used in implicit rejection.
SHAKE128 / SHAKE256 Extendable-output functions (XOF) from the SHA-3 family; squeeze arbitrary bytes; SHAKE128 seeds A expansion (XOF), SHAKE256 used as PRF/G/H per FIPS.
Shared secret (K) The 32-byte KEM output agreed by encapsulator and decapsulator; derived from H(m, H(pk)) on success or PRF(z, c) on failure.
Twiddle factor / Zeta A primitive 2n-th root of unity mod q used in NTT butterfly stages; precomputed into a table of 128 zeta values for n=256.
XOF Extendable-Output Function — SHAKE128 used as the matrix-expansion XOF; takes a seed and (i,j) indices, squeezes coefficients for rejection sampling.
Zeroization Overwriting secret-key and intermediate buffers (s1, s2, t0, y, m) with zeros before deallocation; implemented via the zeroize crate or equivalent barrier write in pqc-ct.
Zq A single coefficient in Z_q; stored as i32 to allow signed intermediates during Montgomery/Barrett arithmetic; always reduced to [0, q) before serialization.

pqc-ntt/src/zetas.rs const ZETAS: [i32; 128]; generated from q, generator.
pqc-ntt/src/ntt.rs ntt_forward, ntt_inverse; Cooley-Tukey / Gentleman-Sande.
pqc-ntt/src/mul.rs ntt_pointwise_mul; ntt_mul (fwd × fwd → pointwise → inv).
pqc-hash/Cargo.toml Manifest; no non-std dependencies by default.
pqc-hash/src/lib.rs Public API: sha3_256, sha3_512, shake128_xof, shake256_xof.
pqc-hash/src/keccak.rs Keccak-f[1600] permutation; 24 rounds; state [u64; 25].
pqc-hash/src/sha3.rs sha3_256, sha3_512; padding; finalize.
pqc-hash/src/shake.rs Shake128, Shake256 XOF structs; absorb/squeeze interface.
pqc-hash/src/fips_names.rs G = sha3_512, H = sha3_256, PRF = shake256, XOF = shake128.
pqc-sample/Cargo.toml Manifest; depends on pqc-hash, pqc-core.
pqc-sample/src/lib.rs Public API: cbd_eta1, cbd_eta2, rej_uniform, expand_a/s/y.
pqc-sample/src/cbd.rs cbd_eta1, cbd_eta2: PRF stream → Poly with small coeffs.
pqc-sample/src/uniform.rs rej_uniform: XOF stream → Poly mod q via rejection.
pqc-sample/src/expand.rs expand_a, expand_s, expand_y from seed/sigma/rho.
pqc-encode/Cargo.toml Manifest; depends on pqc-poly, pqc-core.
pqc-encode/src/lib.rs Public API: bit-pack/unpack, compress/decompress, Dilithium pack/unpack.
pqc-encode/src/bitpack.rs poly_tobytes_d / poly_frombytes_d: d bits/coeff packing.
pqc-encode/src/compress.rs compress_q(x,d), decompress_q(x,d): Kyber rounding.
pqc-encode/src/dilithium_pack.rs Pack/unpack t1, t0, s1, s2, z, h per FIPS 204.
pqc-ct/Cargo.toml Manifest; depends only on pqc-core; no std I/O.
pqc-ct/src/lib.rs Public API: ct_eq, ct_select, ct_cmov, Zeroize impls.
pqc-ct/src/cmp.rs ct_eq_bytes: constant-time byte-slice equality; returns u8 mask.
pqc-ct/src/select.rs ct_select: choose a or b based on mask; no branch.
pqc-ct/src/zeroize.rs SecretBox<T>, ZeroizeOnDrop; barrier + memset pattern.
pqc-kem/Cargo.toml Manifest; depends on pqc-core, pqc-poly, pqc-ntt, pqc-hash, pqc-sample, pqc-encode, pqc-ct.
pqc-kem/src/lib.rs Public API: kem_keygen, kem_encaps, kem_decaps per parameter set.
pqc-kem/src/cpa.rs cpa_keygen, cpa_enc, cpa_dec; Module-LWE CPA PKE.
pqc-kem/src/kem.rs kem_keygen, kem_encaps, kem_decaps; FO; implicit rejection.
pqc-sign/Cargo.toml Manifest; depends on same set as pqc-kem plus sign-specific modules.
pqc-sign/src/lib.rs Public API: sign_keygen, sign, verify per parameter set.
pqc-sign/src/keygen.rs sign_keygen: expand A, sample s1/s2, compute t=As1+s2, split t.
pqc-sign/src/sign.rs sign: Fiat-Shamir with aborts; rejection loop; emit Signature.
pqc-sign/src/verify.rs verify: UseHint, recompute c', norm checks, hint count.
pqc-sign/src/hints.rs make_hint, use_hint: carry-correction bit computation.
pqc-kat/Cargo.toml Manifest; dev/test only; depends on all pqc-* crates.
pqc-kat/src/lib.rs KAT harness crate root; re-exports runner, rng, vectors.
pqc-kat/src/rng.rs DeterministicRng: seeded ChaCha8; reproducible KAT seeds.
pqc-kat/src/runner.rs run_kat_kem, run_kat_sign: parse JSON, call API, assert bytes.
pqc-kat/src/vectors.rs load_vectors(path) -> Vec<KatVector>; JSON → KatVector.
pqc-cli/Cargo.toml Manifest; binary crate; depends pqc-kem, pqc-sign, pqc-kat.
pqc-cli/src/main.rs clap dispatch: keygen | encaps | decaps | sign | verify | kat.
pqc-cli/src/keygen.rs --scheme ml-kem-768|... → write pk/sk files.
pqc-cli/src/encaps.rs Read pk → print ciphertext + shared secret (hex).
pqc-cli/src/decaps.rs Read sk + ciphertext → print shared secret.
pqc-cli/src/sign_cmd.rs Read sk + message → print signature.
pqc-cli/src/verify_cmd.rs Read pk + message + signature → VALID or INVALID.
pqc-cli/src/kat_cmd.rs Run full ACVP suite; summary report; exit 1 on any fail.

Appendix B — Milestone Summary

Phase Deliverable Duration Key Risks Exit Criteria
Phase 1 pqc-core: parameter sets, Zq, Barrett/Montgomery reduction 1 week Off-by-one in Montgomery R selection; i32 overflow in intermediate products Property tests: reduce range [0,q); Montgomery roundtrip; both q values pass
Phase 2 pqc-poly: Poly, PolyVec, PolyMat; schoolbook negacyclic multiply 1 week Negacyclic sign error (X^256≡-1 vs +1); Domain mixing silent bug Schoolbook commutativity; all coefficients after reduce in [0,q); Domain tag enforced
Phase 3 pqc-ntt: forward/inverse NTT, pointwise mul, zeta tables 1.5 weeks Wrong zeta generation (must be primitive 2n-th root); Gentleman-Sande inverse scaling inv(fwd(p))==p; ntt_mul==schoolbook_mul for 1000 random polys; constant-time (no secret branch)
Phase 4 pqc-hash: Keccak-f[1600], SHA3-256/512, SHAKE128/256 XOF 1.5 weeks Theta/rho lane-order bugs; domain separation byte wrong (0x06 vs 0x1f) KAT against NIST SHA-3 competition vectors; multi-block squeeze correct; empty-input edge cases
Phase 5 pqc-sample: CBD, uniform rejection, A/secret expansion 1 week CBD not constant-time on secret; rejection modulus bias; wrong XOF instantiation per spec Distribution tests pass; CBD constant-time (data-independent); seeded expand determinism matches FIPS
Phase 6 pqc-encode: bit-packing, compress/decompress, Dilithium pack 1 week Bit-endian ordering wrong; compression rounding off by 1; Dilithium z sign-extension Roundtrip tests all bit widths; compression error ≤ FIPS bound; malformed-length returns error
Phase 7 pqc-kem CPA PKE: keygen, encrypt, decrypt (Module-LWE) 1.5 weeks NTT-domain matrix multiply produces wrong result; transpose confusion A vs A^T CPA correctness: Dec(sk, Enc(pk, m)) == m; negligible failure rate over parameter sweep
Phase 8 pqc-kem CCA2 KEM: FO transform, implicit rejection 1 week FO comparison branches on secret; wrong G/H input encoding; z used before randomization Encaps→decaps roundtrip; tampered ciphertext returns distinct key; no timing branch on comparison
Phase 9 pqc-sign keygen: expand A, s1/s2, t=A·s1+s2, t1/t0 split 1 week HighBits/LowBits split off-by-one; t0 leaks via encoding; wrong H(pk) computation Keygen KAT vectors match; t1+t0 reconstruction == t; public key size matches FIPS 204 spec
Phase 10 pqc-sign sign: Fiat-Shamir-with-aborts loop, hint computation 2 weeks Infinite rejection loop (bounds too tight); hint overflow (more than omega hints); y not zeroized Sign KAT vectors match; norm bounds enforced; average rejection iterations within expected range
Phase 11 pqc-sign verify: w1' reconstruction, norm check, hint check 1 week UseHint implementation differs from MakeHint inverse; hint-count limit not checked Verify accepts valid signatures; rejects tampered message, key, signature bytes; hint-count bound enforced
Phase 12 pqc-ct: constant-time hardening, zeroization 1 week Compiler optimizes away ct barriers; Zeroize drop order races with borrow ct_eq/select behavioral tests; no secret branch in FO compare; zeroize smoke tests; clippy CT lint pass
Phase 13 pqc-kat: ACVP KAT harness, all 6 parameter sets, public API 1.5 weeks Deterministic RNG shim does not reproduce ACVP seed schedule; API surface changes break KAT All 6 ACVP vector groups pass byte-exact; keygen/encaps/decaps/sign/verify public API stable
Phase 14 Fuzz, perf benchmarks, side-channel review, docs, crate pack 2 weeks Fuzz exposes panic in malformed-key path; timing variance detected on secret path Fuzz: 1M iterations no panic; bench: NTT<1µs/poly, KEM-768<1ms; security review doc complete; crate publishable
Total All 14 phases complete; all 6 parameter sets KAT-passing ~17 weeks Supply-chain audit (deny.toml); FIPS algorithm boundary changes in late drafts All invariants I1–I12 pass; cargo deny check clean; docs.rs renders; no unsafe outside pqc-ct
